<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm: compactness}
	Let $n\geq 2$ and let $(M^{n + 1}, \partial M, \mathrm{g})$ be a smooth complete Riemannian manifold-with-boundary. Consider a sequence of smooth (up to the boundary) embedded two-sided hypersurfaces-with-boundary $\{(\Sigma_k, \partial \Sigma_k)\}_{k}$. Assume the following, for each $k$. 
	\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
		\item \emph{(a priori control on the singular set)} $\mathcal{S}_k=\overline{\Sigma}_k \setminus \Sigma_k$ has locally finite $\mathcal{H}^{n - 2}$-measure, where the closure is taken in $M$, and the embedding is proper in $M\setminus \mathcal{S}_k$. 
		\item \emph{(free boundary condition)} $\partial \Sigma_k  \subset \partial M$.
		\item \emph{(stationarity)} $\Sigma_k$ is minimal in its interior $\Sigma_k \setminus \partial \Sigma_k$, in the sense that its mean curvature vanishes; for every $p\in \partial \Sigma_k$ the tangent half-plane to $\Sigma_k$ at $p$ is orthogonal to $T_p \partial M$. 
		\item \emph{(stability)} for every $\varphi \in C^{0,1}_c(\Sigma_k)$ the stability inequality holds on $\Sigma_k$, namely
        \begin{equation}\label{eq: stability intro}
		  \int_{\Sigma_k} |\nabla_{\Sigma_k} \varphi|^2 - \int_{\Sigma_k}\big(|A_{\Sigma_k}|^2 + \operatorname{Ric}_M(\nu_{\Sigma_k},\nu_{\Sigma_k})\big) \varphi^2  - \int_{\partial \Sigma_k} \mathrm{I\!I}_{\partial M}(\nu_{\Sigma_k}, \nu_{\Sigma_k}) \varphi^2\geq 0,
		\end{equation}
		where $\nu_{\Sigma_k}$ is a continuous determination of a unit normal to $\Sigma_k$.
		\item \emph{(mass bounds)} For every compact set $K \subset M$ we have $\sup_k \mathcal{H}^n(\Sigma_k \cap K) < + \infty$. 
	\end{enumerate}
	Then $\Sigma_k$ converges locally subsequentially in $M$ to a smooth embedded hypersurface-with-boundary $\Sigma$ such that $\mathcal{S}=\overline{\Sigma} \setminus \Sigma$ has $\dim_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{S}) \leq n - 7$; in addition, if $n=7$ the set $\mathcal{S}$ is discrete. The convergence is locally smooth and graphical (possibly with multiplicity) on $M\setminus \mathcal{S}$.
Moreover, the hypersurface $\Sigma$ is a free-boundary minimal hypersurface (i.e. it satisfies (ii)-(iii) with $\Sigma$ in place of $\Sigma_k$) and, when taken with multiplicity, $\Sigma$ is the image of a two-sided stable free-boundary minimal immersion.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
More precisely, $\Sigma$ in \Cref{thm: compactness} (while not being necessarily two-sided) satisfies:
	\begin{itemize}
		\item If $\Gamma$ is a two-sided connected component of $\Sigma$, with unit normal $\nu_\Gamma$, then it satisfies the stability inequality \eqref{eq: stability intro} with $\Gamma$ in place of $\Sigma_k$.
		\item If $\Gamma$ is a one-sided connected component of $\Sigma$, then the inclusion $\Gamma\xhookrightarrow{} M$ lifts to a two-to-one immersion $\tilde{\iota}:\widetilde{\Gamma} \to M$ of the orientable double cover $\widetilde{\Gamma}$ in the normal bundle $N\Gamma$, with image $\Gamma$. Then $\widetilde{\Gamma}$ endowed with the pullback metric $\tilde{\iota}^*\mathrm{g}$ has a globally-defined unit normal $\tilde{\nu}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}$ and the following stability inequality holds
		  \begin{equation*}
		  	\int_{\widetilde{\Gamma}} |\nabla_{\widetilde{\Gamma}} \phi|^2 - \int_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}\big(|A_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}|^2 + \operatorname{Ric}_{M}(\tilde{\nu}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}},\tilde{\nu}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}})\big) \phi^2  - \int_{\partial \widetilde{\Gamma}} \mathrm{I\!I}_{\partial M}(\tilde{\nu}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}, \tilde{\nu}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}) \phi^2\geq 0,
		  \end{equation*}
		  for all $\phi\in C^{0,1}_c(\widetilde{\Gamma})$.
	\end{itemize}
    See \Cref{sec: limit stability} for more details.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rmk: compactness theorem varifold}
Convergence aspects of \Cref{thm: compactness} are naturally formulated in the language of \emph{varifolds}. Indeed, under the assumptions of \Cref{thm: compactness}, each $\Sigma_k$ defines a free-boundary stationary integral varifold $|\Sigma_k|\coloneqq \mathbf{v}(\Sigma_k, \1_{\Sigma_k})$. (We show this in \Cref{sec: capacity and extension of stationarity}, as a consequence of the size assumption on $\mathcal{S}_k$, see also \Cref{sec: smallness intro}.) We then prove that, up to a subsequence, the varifolds $|\Sigma_k|$ converge to the varifold $V=\theta|\Sigma|$, where $\Sigma$ is as in \Cref{thm: compactness}, and $\theta$ is a locally constant integer-valued locally summable positive function on $\Sigma$. In particular, the density function $\theta$ is constant on each connected component of $\Sigma$. Moreover, if $\Gamma$ is a one-sided connected component of $\Sigma$, then $\theta$ is necessarily an even positive integer on $\Gamma$.
Finally, $V$ is free-boundary stationary and stable, i.e. for every vector field\footnote{$\mathfrak{X}^{\mathrm{tan}}(M)=\{X\in C^1_c(M, TM): X(x)\in T_x(\partial M) \text{ for all }x\in \partial M\}$.} $X\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathrm{tan}}(M)$, we have $\delta V(X)=0$ and $\delta^2V(X)\geq 0$. See \Cref{sec: compactness} for details.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rmk: smoothness boundary}
We emphasise that, throughout this paper, every reference to \emph{smoothness} or \emph{smooth convergence} of hypersurfaces-with-boundary should be interpreted as \emph{smoothness up to the boundary} and \emph{smooth convergence up to the boundary} respectively.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{rmk: zero mean curvature limit}
Notice that the limit hypersurface $\Sigma$ in \Cref{thm: compactness} may still touch the boundary in its interior, namely it is possible that $(\Sigma\setminus \partial \Sigma)\cap \partial M\neq \emptyset$. However, since the convergence to $\Sigma$ is smooth and graphical, the mean curvature of $\Sigma$ vanishes on $(\Sigma\setminus \partial \Sigma)\cap \partial M$. This constrains the possible behaviours. For example, by the maximum principle, if $\partial M$ is strictly mean-convex (namely $H_{\partial M}>0$), then necessarily $(\Sigma\setminus \partial \Sigma)\cap \partial M= \emptyset$. Similarly, if $U\subset \partial M$ is a relatively open set and $U\subset(\Sigma\setminus \partial \Sigma)\cap \partial M$, then, again by the maximum principle, $H_{\partial M}\equiv 0$ on $U$.

It is also possible that $(\overline{\Sigma}\setminus (\overline{\partial \Sigma} \cup \Sigma))\cap \partial M \neq \emptyset$, namely that $\overline \Sigma$ touches the boundary at an interior singular point (see  \Cref{example: interior branch point}).
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
Notice that, as in the interior case, the dimension bound $\dim_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{S}) \leq n - 7$ is sharp, as one can see by considering e.g. one half of the Simons cone, namely the area-minimising (and therefore stable) singular free-boundary minimal hypersurface $\Sigma^+=\bigl\{\sum_{j=1}^4 x_j^2=\sum_{j=5}^8 x_j^2;\  x_8\geq 0\bigr\}\subset \mathbb{R}^8_+$, which can be obtained as a smooth (away from its singular set $\mathcal{S}=\{0\}$) limit of a sequence of stable smooth free-boundary minimal hypersurfaces $\Sigma^+_k$. Indeed, it is well-known that the complement $\mathbb{R}^8 \setminus \Sigma$ of the full Simons' cone consists of two connected components $E_+, E_-$, and each of $E_\pm$ is foliated by rescalings $\{\lambda \Gamma_\pm\}_{\lambda>0}$ of a smooth area-minimising hypersurface $\Gamma_\pm$ such that $\lambda \Gamma_\pm \to \Sigma$ as $\lambda\to 0^+$ both in the sense of varifolds, and locally smoothly away from the origin. Since each $\Gamma_\pm$ is smooth and invariant under reflection in $\{x_8=0\}$, then $\Sigma^+_k\coloneqq k^{-1}(\Gamma_+\cap \{x_8\geq 0\})$ is an area-minimising (hence stable) smooth free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $\mathbb{R}^8_+$ and $\Sigma^+_k\to\Sigma^+$ (which has a codimension 7 singular set) as $k\to \infty$.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\label{rmk: curvature estimates}
    In dimensions $3\leq n+1\leq 7$, where the singular set is empty, the compactness theory can equivalently be expressed as a curvature estimate. More precisely, our \Cref{thm: compactness} implies \cite[Theorem 1.1]{Guang2020} under the additional assumption that the stable free boundary minimal hypersurface $\Sigma$ is embedded. We note that the original theorem \cite[Theorem 1.1]{Guang2020} can now be strengthened, due to the first-named author's extension of the Schoen--Simon--Yau estimates to ambient dimension $n+1=7$ and to the recent progress on the stable Bernstein problem. In particular, as a consequence of \cite[Theorem 2]{Bellettini}, the curvature estimate of \cite[Theorem 1.1]{Guang2020} holds for \emph{immersed} stable free boundary minimal hypersurfaces in the full dimensional range $3\leq n+1\leq 7$, and, as a consequence of \cite{stableBernstein4,stableBernstein5,stableBernstein6,stableBernstein7}, the constant $C_1$ in \cite[Theorem 1.1]{Guang2020} can be taken to be independent of the area bound $C_0$.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm: existence}
    For any given compact Riemannian manifold-with-boundary $(M^{n + 1}, \partial M, \mathrm{g})$ with $n\geq 2$, there exists a smooth embedded hypersurface-with-boundary $\Sigma$ that satisfies the following properties.
\begin{itemize}
    \item $\partial \Sigma  \subset \partial M$.
    \item $\Sigma$ is minimal in its interior $\Sigma \setminus \partial \Sigma$ (in the sense that its mean curvature vanishes) and for every $p\in \partial \Sigma$ the tangent half-plane to $\Sigma$ at $p$ is orthogonal to $T_p \partial M$.
    \item $\overline{\Sigma}\setminus \Sigma$ has Hausdorff dimension at most $n-7$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
Similarly to \Cref{rmk: compactness theorem varifold}, in the language of varifolds, the varifold $V=|\Sigma|$ induced by the free-boundary minimal hypersurface $\Sigma$ in \Cref{thm: existence} is free-boundary stationary, namely its first variation $\delta V$ vanishes along every vector field $X\in \mathfrak{X}^{\mathrm{tan}}(M)$. Moreover, the singular set $\operatorname{sing} V$ of $V$ has Hausdorff dimension at most $n-7$.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
\label{rmk: non-empty boundary} 
Note that in \Cref{thm: existence} the set $\operatorname{spt}\Vert V\Vert\cap\partial M$ might be empty, i.e. $V$ might be supported on an optimally regular closed minimal hypersurface contained in the interior of $M$ (see \Cref{subsec: example empty boundary}). However, under additional geometric assumptions on $(M^{n+1},\partial M, \mathrm g)$, for example if $\operatorname{Ric}_{\mathrm g}\geq0$ on $M$ and $\partial M$ is strictly mean-convex, it is possible to guarantee that $V$ is supported on an optimally regular free-boundary minimal hypersurface with non-empty boundary. See \Cref{subsec: Ricci mean convex} for a proof.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm: boundary eps reg}
Let $M$ be as above, $p\in \partial M$, and
let $P$ be a geodesic ball in $\partial M$ centred at $p$. Assume $\rho_0>0$ is such that $P$ has a $\rho_0$-collar neighbourhood $\mathcal{N}^+_{\rho_0}(P)$ in $M$, and 
\begin{equation}\label{eq: geometric control on neighbourhood}
|\nabla_\mathrm{g}^2d_{\partial M}|^2+|\nabla^3_{\mathrm{g}}d_{\partial M}|+|\operatorname{Rm}_{\mathrm{g}}| \leq \Lambda^2\quad \text{ in $\mathcal{N}^+_{\rho_0}(P)$,}
\end{equation}
where $d_{\partial M}$ denotes the distance function from $\partial M$.
There exist $\varepsilon_0>0, R_0>0$ depending only on the dimension $n$ and the geometric constant $\Lambda$ such that the following holds.\\
Let $\delta \in(0, \rho_0/2)$, $R\in(0, R_0)$, $\sigma\in(0,1)$, and let $P_R = B_R^{\partial M}(p)\subset P$. Let $\Sigma$ be a stable singular properly immersed free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $\mathcal{N}^+_{\rho_0}(P_R)$ such that $\overline{\Sigma} \subset \mathcal{N}^+_\delta(P_R)$ and $\partial \Sigma \subset P_R$. If  
\begin{equation*}
\mathrm{E}_{\Sigma, P}(R)^2\coloneqq R^{-n}\int_{\Sigma} |\nabla_\Sigma d_{\partial M}|^2 <(1-\sigma)^n\varepsilon^2 \quad  \text{for some $\varepsilon<\varepsilon_0$},     
\end{equation*}
then
\begin{equation*}
    |\nabla_\Sigma d_{\partial M}|<C_0(1-\sigma)^{-\frac{n}{n+2}}\,\mathrm{E}_{\Sigma, P}(R)^\frac{2}{n+2}, \quad \text{on $\Sigma \cap \mathcal{N}^+_{\rho_0}(P_{\sigma R})$}, 
\end{equation*}
where the constant $C_0 = C_0(n, \Lambda)$ only depends on the dimension $n$ and the constant $\Lambda$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm: boundary sheeting}
Under the assumptions of \Cref{thm: boundary eps reg}, we further conclude that $\partial \Sigma \cap P_{R/2} = \emptyset$, and that there exist an integer $Q \geq 1$ and ordered Lipschitz functions
\[
        0\leq u_1\leq u_2\leq \cdots \leq u_Q, \qquad u_j \colon P_{R/2} \to [0, \delta),
\]
such that
\[
        \overline{\Sigma} \cap \mathcal{N}^+_{\rho_0}(P_{R/2}) = \bigcup_{j=1}^Q F_{\partial M}\bigl(\operatorname{graph}(u_j)\bigr),
\]
where $F_{\partial M}\bigl(\operatorname{graph}(u_j)\bigr)\coloneqq \{F_{\partial M}(y,u_j(y)):y \in P_{R/2}\}$ and $F_{\partial M} \colon P \times [0, \rho_0) \rightarrow M$ is the normal exponential map of $\partial M$ (as defined in \Cref{sec: preliminaries}) restricted to $P$. Furthermore,
\[
        \operatorname{Lip}_P(u_j)\leq C(n, \Lambda)\mathrm{E}_{\Sigma, P}(R)^\frac{2}{n+2}, 
\]
for every $j=1,\ldots, Q$, where the constant $C(n, \Lambda)$ only depends on the dimension $n$ and the geometric constant $\Lambda$ in \eqref{eq: geometric control on neighbourhood}. Moreover,
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
    \item If we further assume $\mathcal{H}^{n - 2}(\mathcal{S}) = 0$, then there exist smooth functions $v_1, v_2, \ldots, v_Q \colon P_{R/2} \rightarrow [0, \delta)$ such that 
    \[
     \overline{\Sigma} \cap \mathcal{N}^+_{\rho_0}(P_{R/2}) = \bigcup_{j=1}^Q F_{\partial M}\bigl(\operatorname{graph}(v_j)\bigr), 
    \]
    and with 
    \[
    \Vert \nabla^2 v_j \Vert_{C^0(P_{R/2})}+R^\alpha[\nabla^2v_j]_{\alpha, P_{R/2}} \leq C(n, \alpha, \Lambda)\left(R^{-1}\mathrm{E}_{\Sigma, P}(R)^\frac{2}{n+2}+1\right)
    \]
    for all $j = 1, 2, \ldots, Q$ and $\alpha\in(0,1)$. In particular, $R\vert A_\Sigma \vert \leq C(n, \Lambda)\left(\mathrm{E}_{\Sigma, P}(R)^{\frac{2}{n+2}}+ R\right)$ on $\Sigma \cap \mathcal{N}^+_{\rho_0}(P_{R/2})$. 
    \item If, on the other hand, we assume that $\Sigma$ is embedded, then $\overline{\Sigma} \cap \mathcal{N}^+_{\rho_0}(P_{R/2}) = \Sigma \cap \mathcal{N}^+_{\rho_0}(P_{R/2})$ and $0 \leq u_1 < u_2 < \ldots < u_Q$ are smooth with estimates
    \[
    \Vert \nabla^2 u_j \Vert_{C^0(P_{R/2})}+R^\alpha[\nabla^2u_j]_{\alpha, P_{R/2}} \leq C(n, \alpha, \Lambda)\left(R^{-1}\mathrm{E}_{\Sigma, P}(R)^\frac{2}{n+2}+1\right)
    \]
    for all $j = 1, 2, \ldots, Q$ and $\alpha \in(0,1)$. Lastly, $R\vert A_\Sigma \vert \leq C(n, \Lambda)\left(\mathrm{E}_{\Sigma, P}(R)^{\frac{2}{n+2}}+ R\right)$ on $\Sigma \cap \mathcal{N}^+_{\rho_0}(P_{R/2})$. 
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
Our boundary-orthogonal sheeting theorem includes, as a special case, the result in \cite[Section 3]{GruterJostIHP86}. Our construction of $Z$ replaces, in some sense, the flattening of $\partial M$ performed in \cite{GruterJostIHP86}. While $M$ is therein locally described in coordinates as a half ball $B^{n+1}_+(0)$, with $\partial M$ identified with $B^n(0) \times \{0\}$, we use (flow lines of) $Z$ and $\nabla^\mathrm{g} d_{\partial M}$ to reach a neighbourhood of $P$ in $M$ (adapted neighbourhood, using our terminology in \Cref{sec: proof orthogonal sheeting}), noting that $Z$ preserves $\partial M$ and $\nabla^\mathrm{g} d_{\partial M}$ is orthogonal to it. Crucially, we keep the ambient metric and the variational information, while \cite{GruterJostIHP86} work with the Euclidean metric in $B^{n+1}_+(0)$ (following \cite{SchoenSimon81}). That choice would not work for our method, since the Euclidean mean curvature of the transformed hypersurface becomes nonzero and its gradient inevitably appears in the estimates, producing error terms that are not controlled by the available hypotheses. On the other hand, the tilt function $\tau$, being built from $Z$, reflects the Riemannian geometry of the ambient boundary; moreover, with the ambient metric and variational hypotheses left unaltered, $\tau$ satisfies a PDE that allows us to successfully carry out the approach of \cite{Bellettini} in our setting. We note that $Z$ and $\nabla^\mathrm{g} d_{\partial M}$ play a similar role, in flavour, to the construction of a so-called orthogonal foliation in \cite{Li-Zhou-max}, used in the context of a maximum principle for free-boundary minimal submanifolds.
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm: orthogonal eps reg}
Let $M$ be as above and let $P$ be a smooth two-sided, embedded, $n$-dimensional half-disc in $M$ meeting $\partial M$ orthogonally along $\partial_0P$. Let the vector field $Z$ be as above, and let $p\in \partial_0P=P\cap \partial M$. Assume $\rho_1>0$ is such that the flow map $F_Z$ is a diffeomorphism from $P_{\rho_1}\times(-\rho_1, \rho_1)$ onto the relatively open neighbourhood  $\mathcal{N}^Z_{\rho_1}(P)$ of $P$ in $M$. Assume that
\begin{equation}\label{eq: geometric control on neighbourhood orthogonal}
	\begin{aligned}
		&\sup_{ P_{\rho_1}}|A_P|^2+\sup_{P_{\rho_1}}|\nabla_P A_P|+\sup_{ P_{\rho_1}}|\nabla^2_PA_P|^{2/3}\leq \Lambda^2,\\  
		&\sup|\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}|^2+\sup|\nabla^3_\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}|+\sup|\nabla^4_\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}|^{2/3}\leq \Lambda^2,\\
		&\sup|\operatorname{Rm}_\mathrm{g}|+\sup|\nabla^{\mathrm{g}}\operatorname{Rm}_\mathrm{g}|^{2/3}+\sup|\nabla^2_{\mathrm{g}}\operatorname{Rm}_\mathrm{g}|^{1/2}\leq \Lambda^2,
	\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
in $\mathcal{N}^Z_{\rho_1}(P)$, where $d_{\partial M}$ denotes the distance to $\partial M$ in $M$, and $A_P$ denotes the second fundamental form of $P$ in $M$. 
Then there exist $\varepsilon_1>0$,  $ \delta_1 \in (0, \rho_1)$ and $R_1>0$ depending only on the dimension $n$ and the geometric constant $\Lambda$ such that the following holds.\\
Let $\delta \in(0, \delta_1)$, $R\in(0, R_1)$, $\sigma\in(0,1)$. Assume $\Sigma$ is a stable singular properly immersed free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $M$ such that $\overline{\Sigma} \subset \mathcal{N}^Z_\delta(P_R)$ and $\partial \Sigma \subset (\partial M \cap \mathcal{N}^Z_{\rho_1}(P))$. If we let $Z^T$ denote the orthogonal projection of $Z$ onto $T\Sigma$, we have that if
\begin{equation*}
	\mathrm{E}^Z_{\Sigma, P}(R)^2\coloneqq R^{-n}\int_{\Sigma} |Z^T|^2 < (1-\sigma)^n\varepsilon^2 \quad \text{for some $\varepsilon<\varepsilon_1$,}
\end{equation*}
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq: nonlinear Z-tilt estimate}
	|Z^T|\leq C_1(n, \Lambda)(1-\sigma)^{-\frac{n}{n+2}}\,\mathrm{E}^Z_{\Sigma, P}(R)^{\frac{2}{n+2}} \quad \text{on $\Sigma \cap \mathcal{N}^Z_{\rho_1}(P_{\sigma R})$},  
\end{equation}
where the constant $C_1 = C_1(n, \Lambda)$ only depends on the dimension $n$ and the constant $\Lambda$.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm: orthogonal sheeting}
    Under the assumptions of \Cref{thm: orthogonal eps reg}, there exist an integer $Q \geq 1$ and ordered Lipschitz functions 
    \begin{equation*}
        u^Z_1 \leq u^Z_2 \leq \ldots \leq u^Z_Q, \qquad u^Z_j \colon P_{R/2} \rightarrow (-\delta, \delta), 
    \end{equation*}
    such that 
    \begin{equation*}
         \overline{\Sigma} \cap \mathcal{N}^Z_{\rho_1}(P_{R/2}) = \bigcup_{j=1}^Q F_Z\bigl(\operatorname{graph}(u^Z_j)\bigr),
    \end{equation*}
    where $F_Z(\operatorname{graph}(u^Z_j)) = \{F_Z(y, u^Z_j(y)) : y \in P_{R/2} \}$ and $F_Z \colon P_{R/2} \times (-\delta, \delta) \rightarrow M$ is the flow map of the vector field $Z$. Furthermore, 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: nonlinear Lip estimate}
    \operatorname{Lip}_P(u^Z_j)\leq C(n, \Lambda)\left(\delta+\mathrm{E}^Z_{\Sigma, P}(R)^\frac{2}{n+2}\right), 
    \end{equation}
    for every $j=1,\ldots, Q$, where the constant $C(n, \Lambda)$ only depends on the dimension $n$ and the geometric constant $\Lambda$ in \eqref{eq: geometric control on neighbourhood orthogonal}. Moreover, 
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
   	\item If we further assume $\mathcal{H}^{n - 2}(\mathcal{S}) = 0$, then there exist smooth functions $v^Z_1, v^Z_2, \ldots, v^Z_Q \colon P_{R/2} \rightarrow (-\delta, \delta)$ such that 
   \[
   \overline{\Sigma} \cap \mathcal{N}^Z_{\rho_1}(P_{R/2}) = \bigcup_{j=1}^Q F_{Z}\bigl(\operatorname{graph}(v^Z_j)\bigr), 
   \]
   and with 
   \[
   \Vert \nabla^2 v^Z_j \Vert_{C^0(P_{R/2})}+R^\alpha[\nabla^2v^Z_j]_{\alpha, P_{R/2}} \leq C(n, \alpha, \Lambda)\left(R^{-1}\left(\mathrm{E}^Z_{\Sigma, P}(R)^\frac{2}{n+2}+ \delta\right)+1\right)
   \]
   for all $j = 1, 2, \ldots, Q$ and $\alpha\in(0,1)$. In particular, 
   \[
    R\vert A_\Sigma \vert \leq C(n, \Lambda)\left( \delta+ R + \mathrm{E}^Z_{\Sigma, P}(R)^{\frac{2}{n+2}}\right)
   \]
   on $\Sigma \cap \mathcal{N}^Z_{\rho_1}(P_{R/2})$. 
   \item If, on the other hand, we assume that $\Sigma$ is embedded, then $\overline{\Sigma} \cap \mathcal{N}_{\rho_1}(P_{R/2}) = \Sigma \cap \mathcal{N}_{\rho_1}(P_{R/2})$, $u^Z_1, \ldots u^Z_Q$ are smooth and
   \[
    u^Z_1<u^Z_2<\dots<u^Z_Q \quad \text{on $P_{R/2}$}.
   \]
   Moreover, the $u^Z_j$ satisfy estimates
   \[
   \Vert \nabla^2 u^Z_j \Vert_{C^0(P_{R/2})}+R^\alpha[\nabla^2u^Z_j]_{\alpha, P_{R/2}} \leq C(n, \alpha, \Lambda)\left(R^{-1}\left(\mathrm{E}^Z_{\Sigma, P}(R)^\frac{2}{n+2}+ \delta\right)+1\right)
   \]
   for all $j = 1, 2, \ldots, Q$ and $\alpha \in(0,1)$. Lastly, $R\vert A_\Sigma \vert \leq C(n, \Lambda)\left(\delta+ R+\mathrm{E}^Z_{\Sigma, P}(R)^{\frac{2}{n+2}}\right)$ on $\Sigma \cap \mathcal{N}^Z_{\rho_1}(P_{R/2})$. 
   \end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{example: interior branch point}
    We will now exhibit a branched minimal immersion with an interior branch point touching the boundary $\partial M$. Consider $x_{3} = u_\pm(x)$, where $x = (x_1, x_2)$, satisfying $\vert u(x) \vert \leq C \vert x \vert^\alpha$. Let $b_R(x) = - R + \sqrt{R^2 - \vert x \vert^2} = - \frac{\vert x \vert^2}{2R} + O(\vert x \vert^4)$ be the upper half-sphere centred at $-Re_3$. If $\alpha > 2$, then $$ u_\pm(x) \geq - c \vert x \vert^\alpha > - \frac{\vert x \vert^2}{2R} + O(\vert x \vert^4) = b_R(x),$$ for $\vert x \vert$ sufficiently small. In particular, $u = \pm c \Re(z^{5/2})$ satisfies this property, but it is harmonic, not minimal. We will use the Weierstrass--Enneper parametrization to circumvent this. Consider $\Phi(\zeta) = (\zeta(1 - \zeta^6), \mathrm{i}\zeta(1 + \zeta^6), 2 \zeta^4),$ and note that $\Phi \cdot \Phi \equiv 0$. In particular,
    \[
    X(\zeta) = \Re \int \Phi(\zeta) \, d\zeta = \Re \left(\frac{\zeta^2}{2} - \frac{\zeta^8}{8}, \mathrm{i} \frac{\zeta^2}{2} + \mathrm{i} \frac{\zeta^8}{8}, \frac{2\zeta^5}{5} \right)  \in \mathbb{R}^3. 
    \]
    The Weierstrass--Enneper theorem (cf. \cite[p.~33]{Colding2011}) shows that $X$ is a branched minimal immersion, and that $\zeta = 0$ is an isolated branch point. Furthermore, writing $X = (X^\prime, X_3)$ with $X^\prime \in \mathbb{R}^2$, we have 
    \[
    X^\prime(\zeta) = \frac{1}{2}(\Re(\zeta^2), -\Im(\zeta^2)) + O(\vert \zeta \vert^8) \qquad \text{and} \qquad X_3(\zeta) = \frac{2}{5}\Re(\zeta^5),
    \]
    so that $\vert X^\prime(\zeta) \vert \simeq \vert \zeta \vert^2$, while $\vert X_3(\zeta) \vert \leq C \vert \zeta \vert^5 \leq C \vert X^\prime(\zeta) \vert^{5/2},$ so that $X_3 \simeq \pm c \Re((x_1 + \mathrm{i} x_2)^{5/2})$ as required. Now, for small enough $\zeta$, there holds 
    \[
    \vert X(\zeta) + Re_3 \vert^2 - R^2 = \vert X^\prime(\zeta) \vert^2 + X_3(\zeta)^2 + 2R X_3(\zeta) \geq c \vert \zeta \vert^4 - C_R \vert \zeta \vert^5 > 0, 
    \]
    with equality holding at $\zeta = 0$. This is a smooth stable immersion $f:S \coloneqq D\setminus \{0\} \to \mathbb{R}^3$, where $D$ is a sufficiently small disc centred at the origin in $\mathbb{R}^2=\mathbb{C}$. Stability follows from Barta's theorem (cf. \cite[Lemma 1.36]{Colding2011}). More precisely, if $\nu(\zeta)$ denotes the upward pointing unit normal, then $\langle \nu(\zeta), e_3 \rangle$ gives a positive Jacobi field. Take then as manifold-with-boundary $\mathbb{R}^3 \setminus B_R(-Re_3)$. We refer to \cite{SimonWicExa} for more examples of stable branched minimal immersions.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
\label{example: boundary branch point}
Let $f\colon S \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^3$ be as in \Cref{example: interior branch point}, so that in particular $f$ is a two-sided stable branched minimal immersion, with $\partial\Sigma=\emptyset$ and discrete branch set $\mathcal B$. Set $\Sigma=f(S)$, $N=\mathbb{R}^3$, and $F:S\times \mathbb{R}_+ \to \mathbb{R}^4_+$, as well as
\[
\widehat\Sigma\coloneqq\Sigma \times\mathbb R_+,\qquad \widehat M\coloneqq N\times\mathbb R_+ = \mathbb{R}_+^4,\qquad F(x,y,s)\coloneqq(f(x),s),
\]
where $\mathbb R_+=[0,\infty)$ and $\widehat g=g+ds^2$. Then $\partial\widehat\Sigma=\Sigma\times\{0\}$ and $ F(\partial\widehat\Sigma)\subset\partial\widehat M.$ For $X\in T_x\Sigma$, and $a\in\mathbb R$, $dF(X,a)=(df(X),a)$ so that $\operatorname{rank}dF=\operatorname{rank}df + 1.$ Hence
\[
\widehat\Sigma_{\mathrm{reg}} =(\Sigma\setminus\mathcal B)\times\mathbb{R}_+, \qquad \mathcal B_F=\mathcal B\times\mathbb R_+.
\]
Thus $\dim\mathcal B_F = 1$, so $\mathcal B_F$ has codimension $2$, and its boundary part is $\mathcal B\times\{0\}$. On the parameter manifold $S\times \mathbb{R}_+$, $F^*\widehat g=f^*g+ds^2.$ If $\nu$ is a unit normal to $f$, then $\widehat\nu=(\nu,0)$ is a unit normal to $F$, and the product connection gives $A_F\bigl((X,a),(Y,b)\bigr)=A_f(X,Y).$ All mixed and Euclidean-factor terms vanish; hence $\operatorname{tr}A_F=\operatorname{tr}A_f=0,$ so that $F$ is minimal. Along $\partial\widehat\Sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}$, the outward conormal of $F$ and the outward normal to $\partial\widehat M$ are both $-\partial_s$. Thus $F$ is free-boundary, see also \Cref{L:reflection stationary}. Since $\partial\widehat M$ is totally geodesic, $|A_F|^2=|A_f|^2,$ and $\operatorname{Ric}_{\widehat M}(\widehat\nu,\widehat\nu) =\operatorname{Ric}_{N}(\nu,\nu).$ Therefore, for $\varphi\in C_c^\infty(\widehat\Sigma_{\mathrm{reg}})$, writing $\varphi_{s}(x) \coloneqq \varphi(x,s)$,
\[
Q_F(\varphi) = \int_{\mathbb R_+} Q_f(\varphi_{s}) \, ds +\int_{\widehat\Sigma_{\mathrm{reg}}} |\partial_s\varphi|^2 \,d\mu_F \geq0.
\]
By splitting a variation into odd and even components with respect to reflection in the totally geodesic boundary, we see that $F$ is free-boundary stable. Stability extends across $\mathcal B_F$ by logarithmic cutoff. Lastly, if the tangent varifold of $f$ at $p$ is $Q|P|$, then that of $F$ at $F(p,0)$ is $Q\vert P\times\mathbb R_+\vert,$ a multiplicity-$Q$ half-hyperplane meeting $\partial\widehat M$ orthogonally.
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor: uniqueness of tangent hyperplanes}
Let $(\Sigma, \partial \Sigma)$ be as in \Cref{def: stable immersion}, and let $x \in \overline{\Sigma}$ be such that a tangent cone (to the stationary varifold $|\Sigma|$ at $x$) is either a half-hyperplane orthogonal to $T_x\partial M$, or the hyperplane $T_x \partial M$ (in both cases, a constant integer multiplicity may be present). Then the tangent cone is unique.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- question-1: body_md -->
\label{q: compactness immersed}
Is there a compactness theory for stable singular properly immersed free-boundary minimal hypersurfaces with a singular set of locally finite $\mathcal{H}^{n-2}$-measure? This seems likely given the present work and the recent substantial advances in the interior theory in $\mathbb{R}^{n+1}$: in \cite{GWangimmersed}, Wang--Zhang  produce a tilt function that allows them to extend the methods and conclusions of \cite{Bellettini}, leading to a sharp $(n-3)$-dimensional bound for the non-branch singular set, and establish a branched sheeting theorem near cones that are unions of hyperplanes with a spine of dimension $n-1$ and $n-2$. 
At the free boundary one must additionally understand boundary branch points and their interaction with the touching set.
<!-- end question-1 -->
<!-- question-2: body_md -->
\label{question: orthogonal sheeting refinement}
Can the boundary-orthogonal $\varepsilon$-regularity and sheeting theorems (\Cref{thm: orthogonal eps reg,thm: orthogonal sheeting}) be proven under the weaker notion of \emph{stability away from the touching set} used in~\cite[Definition~2.2]{GuangWangZhouFiniteness}? The present proof uses the analytic stability inequality for all compactly supported scalar test functions, including across the touching set, and it is not immediate how to recover the same Caccioppoli estimate from the weaker hypothesis.

Notice that, on the other hand, the tilt function used in the boundary-parallel case naturally vanishes on the touching set, and it is an admissible test function for the stability inequality under the weaker notion of stability away from the touching set.
<!-- end question-2 -->
<!-- question-3: body_md -->
Can the sheeting and compactness theorems above be generalised to the capillary setting? This appears to be promising, in light of the present work and of very recent or simultaneous progress on related aspects. A theory for stable minimal hypersurfaces-with-boundary with a given capillary angle in a Euclidean half-space is developed in \cite{GWangcapillary}. Moreover, in the forthcoming work \cite{BellettiniJiang26}, a regularity and compactness theory is established, for stable hypersurfaces whose mean curvature is prescribed by an ambient function on a Riemannian manifold. A combination of the three works is likely to lead to a compactness and regularity theory for capillary hypersurfaces in the presence of a wetting energy and a potential energy.
<!-- end question-3 -->
<!-- question-4: body_md -->
Let $V$ be an $n$-dimensional rectifiable varifold which is stationary away from a relatively closed set $S$ satisfying $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(S)=0$. Must $V$ be stationary across $S$? \Cref{prop: locally finite then global stationarity} gives a positive answer when $S$ has locally finite $\mathcal{H}^{n-2}$-measure. Its proof is critical at that exponent and the construction used there no longer has the required properties under the weaker assumption $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(S)=0$.
<!-- end question-4 -->
<!-- question-5: body_md -->
What structural hypothesis gives an optimal regularity and compactness theory for free-boundary stationary integral varifolds whose regular part is stable? The desired statement would be a free-boundary counterpart of Wickramasekera's interior theory~\cite{Wick14}, allowing stable regular parts without an a priori $\mathcal{H}^{n-2}$ bound on the singular set and excluding the appropriate boundary classical singularities. This question is the subject of ongoing work.
<!-- end question-5 -->
<!-- remark-10: body_md -->
\label{rmk: extension Omega}
We note that, essentially by the collar neighbourhood construction (\cite[Theorems 9.25 and 9.26]{LeeSmooth}), in the case $\partial M \neq \emptyset$, the manifold $M$ can be realised as a proper (i.e. strict) closed subset $\iota(M) \subsetneq \Omega$ of a smooth $(n+1)$-manifold $\Omega$, with $\iota \colon M \rightarrow \Omega$ a smooth proper embedding.
                                                                                         
In other words, $M$ can be seen as a closed subset $M\subset \Omega$ with smooth boundary $\partial M$. Additionally, $\Omega$ can be taken to be smoothly homotopic to the interior of $M$. Furthermore, $\Omega$ admits a (smooth) Riemannian metric that restricts on $M$ to the given one\footnote{This follows essentially from a lemma of Borel, showing that a function is smooth up to the boundary, in the sense defined earlier, if and only if it admits a smooth extension across the boundary, see \cite[p.27]{LeeSmooth}.}. 

Roughly speaking, the collar neighbourhood (which is a subset of $M$ diffeomorphic to $\partial M \times [0, 1)$, with $\partial M \times \{0\}$ identified with $\partial M$) can be doubled, reflected and smoothly attached outside of $M$ along $\partial M$, thus creating $\Omega$.
<!-- end remark-10 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def: fb smooth immersed min hyp}
Given a smooth $n$-dimensional manifold-with-boundary $N$ and a relatively open set $U\subset M$, a smooth  immersion $\iota: (N, \iota^*\mathrm{g}) \to U\subset (M,\mathrm{g})$ is a \emph{free-boundary minimal immersion} in $U$ if 
\begin{enumerate}
	\item $\iota(\partial N) \subset \partial M \cap U$,
	\item its mean curvature vanishes on the interior $N\setminus \partial N$ of $N$,
	\item for all $z\in\partial N$, the inward conormal of $\partial N$ in $N$ at $z$ is mapped to $\mathbf{n}(\iota(z))$.
\end{enumerate}

We shall denote the image $\iota(N)$ of $\iota$ by $\Sigma$, and let $\partial \Sigma \coloneqq \iota(\partial N)$. Then $(\Sigma, \partial \Sigma)$ is said to be a smooth \emph{immersed free-boundary minimal hypersurface} in $(M, \partial M)$. If, furthermore, $\iota$ is a homeomorphism of $N$ onto its image, we will say that $(\Sigma, \partial \Sigma)$ is a smooth \emph{embedded free-boundary hypersurface} in $(M, \partial M)$.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- remark-11: body_md -->
\label{rmk: touching allowed}
Note that the intersection of the interior of $\Sigma$ and $\partial M$ is not necessarily empty as easy examples show. We stress that the orthogonality between $\Sigma$ and $\partial M$ is only imposed at points in $\partial \Sigma$.
<!-- end remark-11 -->
<!-- remark-12: body_md -->
\label{rmk: FB stationarity}
Notice that the conditions in \Cref{def: fb smooth immersed min hyp} imply that the immersion $\iota:N\to M$ is stationary for area with respect to compactly supported variations $(\iota_t \colon N \rightarrow M)_{t\in(-\delta, \delta)}$ which preserve the inclusion $\iota_t(\partial N)\subset \partial M$ for all $t$, as is easily seen from the first variation formula for area\footnote{Notice that the conditions in \Cref{def: fb smooth immersed min hyp} are stronger than stationarity with respect to compactly supported variations which map $\partial N$ into the boundary $\partial M$. Indeed stationarity only implies the vanishing of the mean curvature of $\Sigma$ away from the \emph{touching set} $(\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma)\cap\partial M$, which is not assumed to be empty, as observed in \Cref{rmk: touching allowed}.}.
<!-- end remark-12 -->
<!-- notation-1: body_md -->
\label{not: immersion}
From now on, whenever for $A\subset M$ we write an integral $\int_{\Sigma\cap A}f$, we shall always implicitly assume it is shorthand for the integral $\int_{\iota^{-1}(A)}f\circ \iota\, d\mathcal{H}^n_{\iota^*\mathrm g}$ over $\iota^{-1}(A)$ with respect to the induced metric $\iota^*\mathrm{g}$. Similarly quantities such as the second fundamental form $A_\Sigma$ or a unit normal $\nu_\Sigma$ are to be understood implicitly as quantities on $N$.
<!-- end notation-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def: sing min hyp}
Let $\iota:N \to M$ be a free-boundary minimal immersion as in \Cref{def: fb smooth immersed min hyp} with image $\Sigma = \iota(N)$, and let $\mathcal{S} = \overline{\Sigma} \setminus \Sigma$, where the closure is taken in $M$. We shall say that $\Sigma$ (or, more precisely, $\overline{\Sigma}$) is a \emph{singular properly immersed free-boundary minimal hypersurface} in $M$ if:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item The immersion $\iota$ is proper in $M \setminus \mathcal{S}$, i.e.~for every compact set $K\subset M\setminus \mathcal{S}$ the inverse image $\iota^{-1}(K)$ is compact in $N$.
    \item The measure $\mathcal{H}^n\mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} \Sigma$ is locally finite, in the sense that for any compact $K\subset M$ we have $\mathcal{H}^n(\Sigma \cap K)<\infty$. 
    \item The set $\mathcal{S}\coloneqq \overline{\Sigma}\setminus \Sigma$ has locally finite $\mathcal{H}^{n-2}$-measure (in $M$), i.e.~for any compact $K\subset M$ we have $\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(\mathcal{S} \cap K)<\infty$. 
\end{enumerate}
We shall refer to $\mathcal{S}$ as the \emph{singular set} of $\Sigma$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- remark-13: body_md -->
\label{rmk: area bounds} 
Under the hypotheses of \Cref{def: sing min hyp} the varifold $V=|\Sigma|$ is free-boundary stationary in $M$ in the sense of \Cref{sec: FB stationary varifolds}. More precisely, under those assumptions one can extend stationarity from $M \setminus \mathcal{S}$ to $M$ (thus giving e.g. area-ratio estimates on balls centred on $\mathcal{S}$ by the monotonicity formula), see the discussion in \Cref{sec: capacity and extension of stationarity} (\Cref{prop: locally finite then global stationarity} in particular).
<!-- end remark-13 -->
<!-- remark-14: body_md -->
\label{rmk: true singular set}
As stated, in \Cref{def: sing min hyp} we may have points in $\mathcal{S}$ that are not genuinely singular, e.g.~we take $\Sigma$ to be a punctured equatorial disc in $\mathbb{B}^3$ and $\mathcal{S}$ to be the missing point. Strictly speaking, one should define the singular set $\operatorname{sing}(\Sigma)$ as the set of all $x\in M$ such that for every $r>0$ the intersection of $\overline{\Sigma}$ with the geodesic ball $B_r(x)$ in $\Omega$ is not the image of a smooth free-boundary immersion in $M$ which is \emph{proper} in $B_r(x)$, that is, $\operatorname{sing}(\Sigma)$ is the set of points across which $\Sigma$ cannot be smoothly extended as a proper free-boundary immersion\footnote{The set $\operatorname{sing}(\Sigma)$ corresponds to the \emph{(non-immersed) singular set} of the varifold $|\Sigma|$ induced by $\Sigma$, in the definition of \cite{WicM2}.}. However, clearly $\operatorname{sing} (\Sigma) \subset\mathcal{S}$ and, after extending $\Sigma$ across its removable singularities, one may take $\mathcal{S} = \operatorname{sing} (\Sigma)$.
<!-- end remark-14 -->
<!-- remark-15: body_md -->
We will speak of a smooth properly immersed free-boundary minimal hypersurface when $\mathcal{S} = \emptyset$. In this case the notion reduces to \Cref{def: fb smooth immersed min hyp} with the additional assumption of properness in $M$.
<!-- end remark-15 -->
<!-- remark-16: body_md -->
In our main compactness theorem, cf. \Cref{thm: compactness}, we specialise to (possibly) singular embeddings, in which case the immersion in \Cref{def: sing min hyp} is assumed to be an embedding. We also recall that a proper injective  immersion with closed image is necessarily an embedding.
<!-- end remark-16 -->
<!-- remark-17: body_md -->
When we refer to a singular properly immersed free-boundary minimal hypersurface as being \emph{two-sided} we shall always mean that there exists a continuous global determination of a unit normal (more precisely, that the pullback normal bundle on $N$ is trivial, and a global unit normal is a continuous section of the pullback unit normal bundle on $N$).
<!-- end remark-17 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def: stable immersion}
Let $\Sigma=\iota(N)$ be a singular properly immersed free-boundary minimal hypersurface in the sense of \Cref{def: sing min hyp}. Further assume $\Sigma$ to be two-sided, with unit normal $\nu_\Sigma$ and second fundamental form $A_\Sigma$\footnote{More precisely, $\nu_\Sigma$ and $A_\Sigma$ are tensor fields on $N$ along $\iota$, see \cref{not: immersion}.}. We say that $\Sigma$ is \emph{stable} if the following inequality holds
\begin{equation}\label{eq: stability-sing}
    \int_N |\nabla_N \varphi|^2-(|A_\Sigma|^2+\operatorname{Ric}_M(\nu_\Sigma, \nu_\Sigma))\varphi^2 - \int_{\partial N}\mathrm{I\!I}_{\partial M}(\nu_\Sigma, \nu_\Sigma)\varphi^2\geq 0
\end{equation}
for all $\varphi\in C^{0,1}_c(N)$, where $N$ is endowed with $\iota^* \mathrm{g}$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- remark-18: body_md -->
\label{rmk: intro stability}
It turns out (see \Cref{def: sing min hyp} and the discussion in \Cref{sec: capacity and extension of stationarity}) that the assumptions in \Cref{def: sing min hyp} imply that the singular set $\mathcal{S}$ has \emph{vanishing relative 2-capacity in $\Sigma$}, denoted $\operatorname{cap}_2(\mathcal{S})=0$. Thus, \eqref{eq: stability-sing} extends to any test function of the form $\varphi = \phi \circ \iota$ for $\phi \in C^1_c(M)$. The proof is by approximation using the cutoff functions provided by \Cref{cor: cap} (see \Cref{lemma: trace inequality} for a similar argument).
<!-- end remark-18 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lemma: trace inequality}
Let $\Sigma$ be a two-sided singular free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $M$ such that $\overline{\Sigma}\subset U$. Then if $f$ is a non-negative compactly supported Lipschitz function in $U$, we have 
\begin{equation}\label{eq: trace inequality smooth 1}
	\int_{\partial \Sigma}f \leq \int_{\Sigma}|\nabla_\Sigma f|+\left(\sqrt n\sup_{U}|\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}|\right)\int_\Sigma f.
\end{equation}
For simplicity, write $C_\mathrm{tr}=C_\mathrm{tr}(n, U)\coloneqq \sqrt n\sup_{U}|\nabla^2_\mathrm{g} d_{\partial M}|$.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Let us first give a one-line proof in the case $f$ is supported away from $\mathcal{S}$ (see also \cite[Lemma 2.1]{Edelen2016}), and then extend it to the more general case of $f$ compactly supported in $U$ by a (more or less standard) excision argument based on \Cref{cor: cap}.

Suppose $f$ is supported away from $\mathcal S$. Then by the divergence theorem on the immersed hypersurface $\Sigma$ we have
\[
	\int_{\partial \Sigma} f = \int_{\partial \Sigma} \mathrm{g}(f \nabla^\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}, \eta)= -\int_\Sigma \operatorname{div}_\Sigma(f \nabla^\mathrm{g} d_{\partial M}) =-\int_\Sigma \mathrm{g}(\nabla_\Sigma f, \nabla^\mathrm{g} d_{\partial M})+f\operatorname{tr}_\Sigma(\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}),
\]
where in the last equality we used that the mean curvature of $\Sigma$ vanishes identically. Equation \eqref{eq: trace inequality smooth 1} follows directly.

For a general $f$, let $K\Subset U$ be such that $\operatorname{spt} f\subset K$. By \Cref{cor: cap}, there exist cutoffs $\chi_j\in C_c^{0,1}(U)$ such that $0\leq \chi_j\leq 1$, $\operatorname{spt} \chi_j\subset U\setminus \mathcal{S}$, $\chi_j\to 1$ locally on $\Sigma$, and $\int_{\Sigma}|\nabla_\Sigma\chi_j|^2\to 0$.
Apply \eqref{eq: trace inequality smooth 1} to $f_j\coloneqq \chi_j f\circ \iota$ to get
\[
\begin{aligned}
	\int_{\partial \Sigma} f_j &\leq \int_\Sigma|\nabla_\Sigma(\chi_j f)|+C_\mathrm{tr}\int_\Sigma f_j \leq \int_\Sigma (\chi_j|\nabla_\Sigma f|+f|\nabla_\Sigma\chi_j|)+C_{\mathrm{tr}}\int_\Sigma f_j.
\end{aligned}
\]
Notice that by (ii) of \Cref{def: sing min hyp}, $\Sigma$\footnote{or, more precisely, the measure $\mathcal{H}^n \mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex}\Sigma$.} has locally finite mass, so by the properties of the cutoffs $\chi_j$ we have
\[
\int_\Sigma f|\nabla_\Sigma\chi_j|\leq \|f\|_{L^\infty}\mathcal{H}^n(\Sigma\cap K)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\Sigma\cap K}|\nabla \chi_j|^2\right)^{1/2}\to 0.
\]
The other terms on the right hand side converge by dominated convergence, and 
\[
\int_{\partial \Sigma}f \leq \liminf_j\int_{\partial \Sigma}f_j
\]
by Fatou's lemma. This concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- remark-19: body_md -->
Notice that by taking $f\equiv 1$ in \eqref{eq: trace inequality smooth 1}, we obtain an upper bound for $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\partial \Sigma)$ in terms of the geometry of $U$ and $\mathcal{H}^n(\Sigma)$.
<!-- end remark-19 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lemma: stability absorbed 1}
Let $\Sigma$ be a \emph{stable} singular free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $M$ such that $\overline{\Sigma}\subset U$. For every $\alpha>0$ there is a finite constant $C(n,\alpha, U)$ depending on $n, \alpha$, $\sup_{U}|\operatorname{Ric}_\mathrm{g}|, \sup_{U}|\nabla^2_\mathrm{g} d_{\partial M}|$  such that
	\[
	\int_{\Sigma}|A|^2\zeta^2\leq (1+\alpha)\int_\Sigma|\nabla_\Sigma\zeta|^2+C(n,\alpha, U)\int_\Sigma \zeta^2
	\]
	for all bounded compactly supported Lipschitz functions $\zeta$ on $U$. More precisely, $C(n,\alpha,U)$ can be taken to be $\sup_U|\operatorname{Ric}_{\mathrm{g}}|+(4\alpha^{-1}+\sqrt n)\sup_{U}|\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}|^2$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Let us first consider the case where $\zeta$ is supported away from $\mathcal{S}$. Then \eqref{eq: stability-sing} holds. 
Therefore, by \Cref{lemma: trace inequality} and Young's inequality
\[
	\left|\int_{\partial \Sigma} \mathrm{I\!I}_{\partial M}(\nu, \nu) \zeta^2\right|\leq \alpha \int_\Sigma |\nabla_\Sigma\zeta|^2+ \|\mathrm{I\!I}_{\partial M}\|_{L^\infty}\left(\alpha^{-1}\|\mathrm{I\!I}_{\partial M}\|_{L^\infty}+C_\mathrm{tr}\right)\int_\Sigma \zeta^2.
\]
This proves the result for compactly supported bounded Lipschitz functions $\zeta$ with $\operatorname{spt} \zeta\subset U\setminus\mathcal{S}$.
	
For the general case, it is enough to apply the estimate we have just obtained to $\zeta_j\coloneqq \chi_j \zeta\in C_c^{0,1}(U\setminus\mathcal{S})$, where $\chi_j$ is as in the proof of \Cref{lemma: trace inequality}.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lemma: Sobolev inequality FB 1}
Let $\Sigma$ be a two-sided singular free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $M$ such that $\overline{\Sigma}\subset U$.
There are a dimensional constant $C_{\mathrm{Sob}}=C_{\mathrm{Sob}}(n)>0$ and a geometric constant $K_{\mathrm{Sob}}=K_{\mathrm{Sob}}(n,U)$ depending on $n$ and $\sup_{U}|\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}|>0$ such that for all compactly supported Lipschitz functions $\zeta$ on $U$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq: Sobolev inequality 3d 1}
	\left(\int_\Sigma |\zeta|^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}\right)^{\frac{n-2}{n}}\leq C_{\mathrm{Sob}}\left(\int_\Sigma|\nabla_\Sigma \zeta|^2+K_{\mathrm{Sob}}^2\int_\Sigma\zeta^2\right)\quad \text{for $n\geq 3$},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq: Sobolev inequality 2d 1}
	\left(\int_\Sigma |\zeta|^{4}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C_{\mathrm{Sob}}
	\left(\int_\Sigma \zeta^2\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_\Sigma |\nabla_\Sigma \zeta|^2+K_{\mathrm{Sob}}^2 \int_\Sigma\zeta^2\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad \text{for $n= 2$}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
For $\zeta$ supported away from $\mathcal{S}$, this follows from the free-boundary Michael-Simon inequality in \cite[Theorem 2.2]{Edelen2016} and the trace inequality \eqref{eq: trace inequality smooth 1}
applied to $f=|\zeta|^{\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}}$ for $n\geq 3$ and $f=\zeta^2$ for $n=2$, followed by Young's inequality. 

The general case then follows from the same approximation argument as in \Cref{lemma: stability absorbed 1} using \Cref{cor: cap}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lemma: abstract DG}
Let $\Sigma$, $u$ be as above and let $\sigma\in(0,1)$ be fixed. There exists $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{DG}}=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{DG}}(n, k_*, C_{\mathrm{Sob}}, C_\mathrm{Cac}, C_c)>0$ with the following property.
Let $\mathcal{d}\in(0,k_*]$.
If $K_*R\leq 1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq: DG initial estimate}
	R^{-n}\int_{U_R}u^2\leq \varepsilon_{\mathrm{DG}}^2(1-\sigma)^n \mathcal{d}^{n+2},
\end{equation}
then 
\begin{equation}\label{eq: DG conclusion}
	\sup_{U_{\sigma R}}u\leq \mathcal{d}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Define
\[
	k_\ell=\mathcal{d}\left(1-\frac{1}{2^\ell}\right), \quad r_\ell= \sigma R+2^{-\ell}(1-\sigma)R
\]
for $\ell\geq 0$. Hence $k_0=0$ and $k_\ell\uparrow \mathcal{d}\leq k_*$,  $r_0=R$ and $r_\ell\downarrow \sigma R$.
	
Since $k_\ell\leq k_*$, we can apply \eqref{eq: abstract Caccioppoli}. Let $U_\ell\coloneqq U_{r_\ell}$, $u_\ell\coloneqq (u-k_\ell)_+$, and $D_\ell\coloneqq U_\ell \cap \{u>k_\ell\}$. Then let 
\[
	G_\ell\coloneqq \int_{\Sigma\cap U_\ell} u_\ell^2.
\]
Notice that, by definition (since both the cylinders and the truncations decrease), 
\[
G_{\ell+1}\leq G_\ell.
\]
Now let $\phi_\ell\coloneqq \phi_{r_{\ell+1}, r_\ell}$, so that 
\[
0\leq \phi_\ell\leq 1, \quad \phi_\ell\equiv 1 \text{ on } U_{\ell+1}, \quad \operatorname{spt} \phi_\ell\subset U_{\ell}, \quad |\nabla_\Sigma \phi_\ell|\leq \frac{C_c}{(1-\sigma)R}2^{\ell+1},
\]
where $C_c$ is the fixed constant from the setup.

On $D_{\ell+1}$, by definition, we have $u>k_{\ell+1}$, so that
\[
	1-\frac{k_\ell}{u}\geq \frac{k_{\ell+1}-k_\ell}{k_{\ell+1}}\geq 2^{-\ell-1} \quad \text{on $D_{\ell+1}$}.
\]
Therefore,
\[
2^{-\ell-1}\int_\Sigma\phi_\ell^2|\nabla_\Sigma u_{\ell+1}|^2 = 2^{-\ell-1}\int_{U_{\ell}\cap\{u>k_{\ell+1}\}}\phi_\ell^2|\nabla_\Sigma u|^2\leq \int_{D_\ell}\left(1-\frac{k_\ell}{u}\right)|\nabla_\Sigma u|^2\phi_\ell^2,
\]
and, by \eqref{eq: abstract Caccioppoli}, since $|\nabla_\Sigma\phi_\ell|^2\leq C^2_c4^\ell ((1-\sigma)R)^{-2}$ and $\operatorname{spt} \phi_\ell\subset U_\ell$, we obtain 
\[
\begin{aligned}
\int_\Sigma \phi_\ell^2|\nabla_\Sigma u_{\ell+1}|^2 &\leq 2^{\ell+1}C_{\mathrm{Cac}}\left(C^2_c\,4^{\ell+1}((1-\sigma)R)^{-2}G_\ell+K^2_{\mathrm{Cac}}\,\mathcal{H}^n(D_\ell)\right)\\
&\leq C_08^\ell\left(((1-\sigma)R)^{-2}G_\ell+K^2_{\mathrm{Cac}}\,\mathcal{H}^n(D_\ell)\right),
\end{aligned}
\]
where, throughout this proof, $C_0$ denotes a constant which is allowed to change from line to line depending on $n, k_*, C_{\mathrm{Cac}}, C_\mathrm{Sob}, C_c$ only.

Since $|\nabla_\Sigma(\phi_\ell u_{\ell+1})|^2\leq 2\phi^2_\ell|\nabla_\Sigma u_{\ell+1}|^2+2u^2_{\ell+1}|\nabla_\Sigma \phi_\ell|^2$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq: abstract energy estimate}
	\int_\Sigma |\nabla(\phi_\ell u_{\ell+1})|^2\leq C_08^\ell \left(((1-\sigma)R)^{-2}G_\ell+K^2_{\mathrm{Cac}}\,\mathcal{H}^n(D_\ell)\right).
\end{equation}

Notice  that on $D_\ell$ we have $u_{\ell-1}=u-k_{\ell-1}>k_\ell-k_{\ell-1}\geq \mathcal{d}2^{-\ell}$, so that by Markov's inequality
\begin{equation}\label{eq: abstract Markov estimate}
\mathcal{H}^n(D_\ell)\leq \left(\frac{2^\ell}{ \mathcal{d}}\right)^2\int_{D_\ell}u_{\ell-1}^2\leq \left(\frac{2^\ell}{ \mathcal{d}}\right)^2\int_{U_{\ell-1}}u_{\ell-1}^2\leq 4^\ell \mathcal{d}^{-2} G_{\ell-1},
\end{equation}
as $D_\ell\subset U_\ell\subset U_{\ell-1}$.
Since $u_{\ell+1}\leq u_\ell$, $G_\ell\leq G_{\ell-1}$ and $\mathcal{d}\leq 1$, by \eqref{eq: abstract energy estimate} and \eqref{eq: abstract Markov estimate}, we obtain 
\begin{equation}\label{eq: energy+Markov}
\begin{aligned}	
	\int_\Sigma |\nabla_\Sigma(u_{\ell+1}\phi_\ell)|^2+K_{\mathrm{Sob}}^2\int_\Sigma u^2_{\ell+1}\phi_\ell^2 &\leq C_08^\ell\left(((1-\sigma)R)^{-2}G_\ell+4^\ell\mathcal{d}^{-2} K_*^2G_{\ell-1}\right)+K_*^2G_\ell\\
	&\leq C_032^\ell ((1-\sigma)R)^{-2}\mathcal{d}^{-2}G_{\ell-1},
\end{aligned}	
\end{equation}
where in the last inequality we used the condition $K_*R\leq 1$.

\noindent{\bf Case $n\geq 3$}: Assume $n\geq 3$, and apply the Sobolev inequality in \Cref{lemma: Sobolev inequality FB 1} to obtain 
\[
\begin{aligned}
	\left(\int_\Sigma |u_{\ell+1}\phi_\ell|^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}\right)^{\frac{n-2}{n}} &\leq C_{\mathrm{Sob}}\left(\int_\Sigma|\nabla_\Sigma(u_{\ell+1}\phi_\ell)|^2+K_{\mathrm{Sob}}^2\int_\Sigma u_{\ell+1}^2\phi_\ell^2\right)\\
	&\leq C_{\mathrm{Sob}}\left(\int_\Sigma|\nabla_\Sigma(u_{\ell+1}\phi_\ell)|^2+K_{\mathrm{Sob}}^2G_\ell\right).
\end{aligned}
\]
Since $u_{\ell+1}\leq u_\ell$ and $\operatorname{spt}\phi_\ell\subset U_\ell$ and $\phi_\ell\equiv 1$ on $U_{\ell+1}$, then, by \eqref{eq: energy+Markov}
\begin{equation}\label{eq: abstract Markov 1}
	\left(\int_{U_{\ell+1}} u_{\ell+1}^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}\right)^{\frac{n-2}{n}} \leq C_032^\ell((1-\sigma)R)^{-2}\mathcal{d}^{-2} G_{\ell-1}.
\end{equation}
By H\"older's inequality and \eqref{eq: abstract Markov estimate}
\begin{equation}\label{eq: abstract iteration 2step}
	\begin{aligned}
		G_{\ell+1} = \int_{D_{\ell+1}}u_{\ell+1}^2 &\leq \left(\int_{U_{\ell+1}} u_{\ell+1}^{\frac{2n}{n-2}}\right)^{\frac{n-2}{n}}\mathcal{H}^n(D_{\ell+1})^{\frac{2}{n}}\\
		&\leq C_032^\ell ((1-\sigma)R)^{-2}\mathcal{d}^{-2} G_{\ell-1}( 4^{\ell+1}\mathcal{d}^{-2}G_\ell)^\frac{2}{n}\\
		&\leq C_0 128^\ell ((1-\sigma)R)^{-2}\mathcal{d}^{-2-\frac{4}{n}} G_{\ell-1}^{1+\frac{2}{n}}.
	\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\bigskip

\noindent {\bf Case $n=2$}:
For $n=2$, the Sobolev inequality in \Cref{lemma: Sobolev inequality FB 1} gives 
\[
\begin{aligned}
	\left(\int_\Sigma u_{\ell+1}^4\phi_\ell^{4}\right)^{1/2} &\leq C_{\mathrm{Sob}}\left(\int_\Sigma u_{\ell+1}^2\phi_\ell^2\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_\Sigma |\nabla_\Sigma (u_{\ell+1}\phi_\ell)|^2+ K_{\mathrm{Sob}}^2\int_\Sigma u_{\ell+1}^2\phi_\ell^2\right)^{1/2}
\end{aligned}		
\]
so that
\[
\begin{aligned}
	\left(\int_{U_{\ell+1}}u^4_{\ell+1}\right)^{1/2} &\leq C_{\mathrm{Sob}}G_\ell^{1/2}\left(C_032^\ell((1-\sigma)R)^{-2}\mathcal{d}^{-2} G_{\ell-1}\right)^{1/2}\\
	&\leq C_032^\ell((1-\sigma)R)^{-1}\mathcal{d}^{-1}G_{\ell-1}.
\end{aligned}
\]
By H\"older's inequality,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}\label{eq: abstract iteration 2step 2d}
	G_{\ell+1} &\leq \left(\int_{U_{\ell+1}}u_{\ell+1}^4\right)^{1/2}\mathcal{H}^n(D_{\ell+1})^{1/2}\\
	&\leq \left(C_032^\ell((1-\sigma)R)^{-1}\mathcal{d}^{-1}G_{\ell-1}\right)\left(4^{\ell+1}\mathcal{d}^{-2}G_\ell\right)^{1/2}\\
	&\leq C_0 128^\ell((1-\sigma)R)^{-1}\mathcal{d}^{-2}G_{\ell-1}^{3/2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

Notice that \eqref{eq: abstract iteration 2step} and \eqref{eq: abstract iteration 2step 2d} are of the same form. More concretely, if we set 
\[
p_n\coloneqq \begin{cases}
	\frac{2}{n} &\text{if $n\geq 3$,}\\
	\frac{1}{2}	&\text{if $n=2$,}
\end{cases}
\]
and $S_j\coloneqq ((1-\sigma)R)^{-n}\mathcal{d}^{-n-2}G_{2j}$, then by letting $\ell=2j+1$ in  \eqref{eq: abstract iteration 2step} and \eqref{eq: abstract iteration 2step 2d} respectively, we obtain
\[
	S_{j+1}\leq C_0 128^{2j+1} S^{1+p_n}_{j}
\]
for $j\geq 0$. The standard nonlinear iteration lemma (see \cite[Lemma B.1]{Bellettini}) implies that there exists a constant $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{DG}}=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{DG}}(n, k_*, C_\mathrm{Sob}, C_{\mathrm{Cac}}, C_c)>0$ such that $S_j\to 0$ as $j\to \infty$ provided $S_0\leq \varepsilon_{\mathrm{DG}}^2$.
Since $\{G_\ell\}_\ell$ is a non-increasing sequence, this means that
\[
G_\ell\to 0\, \text{as $\ell\to\infty$, provided that } G_0\leq \varepsilon_{\mathrm{DG}}^2 ((1-\sigma)R)^n \mathcal{d}^{n+2},
\]
as required.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor: abstract DG cor}
Under the assumptions of \Cref{lemma: abstract DG}, let 
\[
	\mathrm{E}(R)\coloneqq \left(R^{-n}\int_{U_R}u^2\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.
\]
Then there exist $\varepsilon_*=\varepsilon_*(n, k_*,  C_\mathrm{Sob}, C_{\mathrm{Cac}}, C_c)>0$ and $C_*=C_*(n, k_*, C_\mathrm{Sob}, C_{\mathrm{Cac}}, C_c)>0$ such that if 
\[
	K_*R\leq 1\quad \text{and } \mathrm{E}(R)\leq \varepsilon_*(1-\sigma)^{n/2},
\]
then $\displaystyle{\sup_{U_{\sigma R}}u\leq C_*(1-\sigma)^{-\frac{n}{n+2}}\,\mathrm{E}(R)^{\frac{2}{n+2}}}$.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
This follows from \Cref{lemma: abstract DG}. Indeed, let $\varepsilon_*=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{DG}}k_*^{\frac{n+2}{2}}$ and $C_*\coloneqq  \varepsilon_{\mathrm{DG}}^{-\frac{2}{n+2}}$. Then, if $\mathrm{E}(R)\leq \varepsilon_*(1-\sigma)^{n/2}$, take $\mathcal{d}\coloneqq \varepsilon_{\mathrm{DG}}^{-\frac{2}{n+2}}(1-\sigma)^{-\frac{n}{n+2}}\,\mathrm{E}(R)^{\frac{2}{n+2}}$ and apply \Cref{lemma: abstract DG}.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{def: tilt}
	Let $X\coloneqq \nabla_\Sigma d_{\partial M}$, $g=|X|_\mathrm{g}$, and $\theta\coloneqq \mathrm{g}(\nu, \nabla^\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M})$. We shall refer to $g$ as the \textit{tilt function} on $\Sigma$ with respect to $\partial M$.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lemma: diff ineq tilt}
Let $(M,\mathrm{g})$ and $P_R$ be as above. Additionally, let $\Sigma$ be a smooth two-sided free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $M$ as in \Cref{sec: preliminaries} and assume that $\Sigma\subset \mathcal{N}^+_\delta(P_R)$. Let $\nu$ be a choice of unit normal to $\Sigma$, $A$ its second fundamental form, and let $X, g, \theta$ be as in \Cref{def: tilt}. Then $g^2+\theta^2=1$. Set
$B\coloneqq \nabla^2_\mathrm{g} d_{\partial M}|_{T\Sigma}$. Then the following hold.

First, on $\{g>0\}$, if $v\coloneqq X/g$, then
\[
\nabla_\Sigma g=\theta A(\cdot,v)+B(\cdot,v)
\]
and, for every $\gamma>0$,
\[
|\nabla_\Sigma g|^2 \leq (1+\gamma)(1-g^2)|A(\cdot,v)|^2 + C(\gamma) |B|^2.
\]
Second, assuming that \eqref{eq: geometric bounds boundary} holds, then for every $\gamma>0$ on $\{g>0\}$
\[
\begin{aligned}
	g\Delta_\Sigma g
	&\geq (1-\gamma)|A|^2(1-g^2) - \left(\frac{n-1}{n}+\gamma\right)|A|^2 - \gamma|A|^2 g^2 - C(n,\gamma)\Lambda^2.
\end{aligned}
\]
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Since $d_{\partial M}$ is the signed $\mathrm{g}$-distance to $P$ in $\mathcal{N}^+_{\rho_0}(P)$, we have
$|\nabla^{\mathrm{g}} d_{\partial M} |_{\mathrm{g}}=1$ in the tubular neighbourhood. Thus $\nabla^{\mathrm{g}} d_{\partial M}=X+\theta\nu$ along $\Sigma$, and so $g^2+\theta^2=1$.

The identity $\nabla_\Sigma^2d_{\partial M}=B+\theta A$ follows from the relation between
ambient and intrinsic Hessians. Indeed, for $Y,Z\in T\Sigma$,
\[
        \nabla_\Sigma^2 d_{\partial M}(Y,Z) = \nabla^2_{\mathrm{g}} d_{\partial M}(Y,Z) + \mathrm{g}(\nabla^{\mathrm{g}}d_{\partial M},\nu)A(Y,Z) = B(Y,Z)+\theta A(Y,Z).
\]
By taking $Z=X$ in this identity, we immediately obtain that
\[
g\, Yg = \frac{1}{2}Y(g^2)=\nabla_\Sigma^2d_{\partial M}(Y, X)=B(Y,X)+\theta A(Y,X).
\]
On $\{g>0\}$, writing $X=gv$, this becomes $Yg=\theta A(Y,v)+B(Y,v)$ as required.

Moreover, taking norms and using $\theta^2=1-g^2$, we obtain
\[
        |\nabla_\Sigma^2 d_{\partial M}|^2 = |A|^2(1-g^2)+2\theta\langle A,B\rangle+|B|^2.
\]
The classical Bochner formula on $\Sigma$ gives
\[
        \Delta_\Sigma\frac{g^2}{2} = |\nabla_\Sigma^2 d_{\partial M}|^2 + \langle\nabla_\Sigma\Delta_\Sigma d_{\partial M},X\rangle + \operatorname{Ric}_\Sigma(X,X).
\]
Since $\Delta_\Sigma(g^2/2)=g\Delta_\Sigma g+|\nabla_\Sigma g|^2$, we get
\[
        g\Delta_\Sigma g = |\nabla_\Sigma^2 d_{\partial M}|^2 + \langle\nabla_\Sigma\Delta_\Sigma d_{\partial M},X\rangle + \operatorname{Ric}_\Sigma(X,X) - |\nabla_\Sigma g|^2.
\]
The contracted Gauss equation gives, since $H=0$,
\[
        \operatorname{Ric}_\Sigma(X,X) = \operatorname{Ric}_{\mathrm{g}}(X,X) - \operatorname{Rm}_{\mathrm{g}}(X,\nu,X,\nu) - |A(\cdot, X)|^2.
\]
Combining this with the expansion of $|\nabla_\Sigma^2 d_{\partial M}|^2$ yields the exact Bochner identity.

Now let $\gamma>0$. From the identity $\nabla_\Sigma g=\theta A(\cdot, v)+B(\cdot, v)$ and Young's inequality, we obtain 
\[
|\nabla_\Sigma g|^2 \leq (1+\gamma)(1-g^2)|A(\cdot, v)|^2 + C(\gamma) |B|^2.
\]
By definition, $|A(\cdot, X)|^2=g^2|A(\cdot, v)|^2$, so that 
\[
|\nabla_\Sigma g|^2 + |A(\cdot, X)|^2 \leq (1+\gamma(1-g^2))|A(\cdot, v)|^2 + C(\gamma) |B|^2.
\]
Since $H=0$, $A$ is trace-free. Hence, for every unit vector $v$, we have $|A(\cdot, v)|^2\leq \frac{n-1}{n}|A|^2$.  Therefore,
\[
|\nabla_\Sigma g|^2 + |A(\cdot, X)|^2 \leq \left(\frac{n-1}{n}+\gamma\right)|A|^2 + C(\gamma) |B|^2.
\]
Again using Young's inequality, we also bound
\[
2|\theta\langle A,B\rangle| \leq \gamma |A|^2\theta^2+C(\gamma) |B|^2 = \gamma |A|^2(1-g^2)+C(\gamma) |B|^2,
\]
so that, after substituting into the exact Bochner identity, we obtain
\[
\begin{aligned}
	g\Delta_\Sigma g
	&\geq (1-\gamma)|A|^2(1-g^2) - \left(\frac{n-1}{n}+\gamma\right)|A|^2 - C(\gamma) |B|^2 \\
	&\quad 
	+\langle\nabla_\Sigma\Delta_\Sigma d_{\partial M},X\rangle
	+\operatorname{Ric}_{\mathrm{g}}(X,X)-\operatorname{Rm}_{\mathrm{g}}(X,\nu,X,\nu).
\end{aligned}
\] 
Finally, since $\Sigma$ is minimal, $\Delta_\Sigma d_{\partial M} = \operatorname{tr}_\Sigma \nabla^2_{\mathrm{g}} d_{\partial M} = \Delta_{\mathrm{g}} d_{\partial M} - \nabla^2_{\mathrm{g}} d_{\partial M}(\nu,\nu)$.
Differentiating this expression tangentially gives terms involving
$\nabla^2_{\mathrm{g}}d_{\partial M}$, $\nabla^3_{\mathrm{g}}d_{\partial M}$, and one derivative of $\nu$, hence one factor of $|A|$, namely
\[
	\left| \langle\nabla_\Sigma\Delta_\Sigma d_{\partial M},X\rangle \right| \leq (C(n) |\nabla^3_\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}|+2 |A||\nabla^2_\mathrm{g} d_{\partial M}|)g.
\]
By Young's inequality,
$2 |A||\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}|g\leq \gamma |A|^2g^2+C(\gamma)|\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}|^2$. The curvature terms can be estimated by
\[
\left| \operatorname{Ric}_{\mathrm{g}}(X,X)-\operatorname{Rm}_{\mathrm{g}}(X,\nu,X,\nu) \right|
\leq C(n)|\operatorname{Rm}_\mathrm{g}| g^2.
\]
Assuming \eqref{eq: geometric bounds boundary} holds, this gives the final estimate, since $0\leq g\leq 1$ by definition.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lemma: boundary absorption}
Let $\Sigma, M$, $d_{\partial M}$, and $g$ be as in \Cref{lemma: diff ineq tilt}. Let $\eta$ be the inward unit conormal to $\partial\Sigma$ in $\Sigma$. Then 
\[
	g=1\quad \text{and} \quad\partial_\eta g=0\quad \text{on $\partial\Sigma$}.
\]
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
By definition of a free-boundary minimal hypersurface, $\eta$ coincides with the normal $\mathbf{n}$ to $\partial M$ along $\partial \Sigma$. Since $P_R$ is a relatively open subset of  $\partial M$ and $\partial \Sigma \subset P_R$, then $\eta=\nabla^\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}|_{\partial \Sigma}$.

Hence, $\nabla^\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}$ is tangential to $\Sigma$ along $\partial \Sigma$ and 
so, by definition, $\nabla^\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}|_{\partial\Sigma}=\nabla_\Sigma d_{\partial M}|_{\partial\Sigma}$. In particular, then, for every $x\in \partial\Sigma$
\[
	g(x)=|\nabla_\Sigma d_{\partial M}(x)|=|\nabla^\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}(x)|=1,
\]
i.e. $g|_{\partial \Sigma}\equiv 1$. Since $g^2+\theta^2=1$ everywhere on $\Sigma$, then $\theta|_{\partial\Sigma}\equiv 0$. Thus, on $\partial \Sigma$
\[
	\partial_\eta g=g\partial_\eta g=\frac{1}{2}\partial_\eta g^2=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_\eta \theta^2=-\theta \partial_\eta \theta=0
\]
as $\theta$ vanishes on $\partial \Sigma$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lemma: Caccioppoli inequality 1}
Let $\Sigma$ be a stable \emph{singular} free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $M$ and assume that  $\overline{\Sigma}\subset \mathcal{N}^+_\delta(P_R)$. Assume that \eqref{eq: geometric bounds boundary} holds. Then there exists a positive dimensional constant $C(n)>0$ such that for every $k\in[0,1/(4n)]$ and every $\varphi\in C_c^{0,1}(\mathcal{N}^+_{\rho_0}(P_R))$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq: Caccioppoli smooth A 1}
	\begin{aligned}
		\frac{1}{2n}
		\int_{\{g>k\}}
		|A|^2
		\left(1-\frac{k}{g}\right)\varphi^2
		&\leq
		C(n)\left(\int_\Sigma (g-k)_+^2|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2 + \Lambda^2\int_{\{g>k\}}\varphi^2\right).
	\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq: Caccioppoli smooth nabla 1}
	\begin{aligned}
		\int_{\{g>k\}}
		|\nabla_\Sigma g|^2
		\left(1-\frac{k}{g}\right)\varphi^2
		&\leq
		C(n)\left(\int_\Sigma (g-k)_+^2|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2
		+ \Lambda^2\int_{\{g>k\}}\varphi^2\right).
	\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
We shall first prove \eqref{eq: Caccioppoli smooth A 1} and \eqref{eq: Caccioppoli smooth nabla 1} for test functions $\varphi\in C^{0,1}_c(\Sigma)$, and then extend to compactly supported ambient Lipschitz functions by an excision argument, similar to the one in \Cref{lemma: trace inequality}.

Let $\Omega_k\coloneqq \{g>k\}$. By \Cref{lemma: stability absorbed 1} for every compactly supported Lipschitz function $\zeta$ on $\Sigma$ and a fixed $\alpha>0$ to be determined later, we have
\[
	\int_{\Sigma}|A|^2\zeta^2\leq (1+\alpha)\int_\Sigma|\nabla_\Sigma\zeta|^2+C(n,\alpha)\Lambda^2\int_\Sigma \zeta^2
\]
where $C(n,\alpha)$ only depends on $n, \alpha$.
Let now $\zeta=(g-k)_+\varphi$ to get
\[
	\int_{\Sigma}|A|^2(g-k)_+^2\varphi^2\leq (1+\alpha)\int_\Sigma|\nabla_\Sigma((g-k)_+\varphi)|^2+C(n,\alpha)\Lambda^2\int_\Sigma (g-k)_+^2\varphi^2.
\]
After integration by parts, we have
\[
	\int_\Sigma |\nabla_\Sigma((g-k)_+\varphi)|^2 = -\int_{\Omega_k}(g-k)\Delta_\Sigma g\,\varphi^2 + \int_\Sigma (g-k)_+^2|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2, 
\]
and the boundary term vanishes by \Cref{lemma: boundary absorption}. Hence,
\[
\begin{aligned}
	\int_{\Omega_k}|A|^2(g-k)^2\varphi^2 &\leq -(1+\alpha)\int_{\Omega_k}(g-k)\Delta_\Sigma g\,\varphi^2+(1+\alpha)\int_\Sigma(g-k)^2_+|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2\\
	&\quad +C(n,\alpha)\Lambda^2\int_\Sigma(g-k)^2_+\varphi^2.
\end{aligned}
\]
Notice that on $\Omega_k$, one has
\[
	(g-k)\Delta_\Sigma g=\left(1-\frac{k}{g}\right)g\Delta_\Sigma g,
\]
so we can use the differential inequality for the tilt in \Cref{lemma: diff ineq tilt} to obtain for $\gamma>0$ to be determined later
\[
	\begin{aligned}
		-(g-k)\Delta_\Sigma g \leq -\left(1-\frac{k}{g}\right)\left[\left((1-\gamma)(1-g^2)-\frac{n-1}{n}-\gamma-\gamma g^2\right)|A|^2-C(n,\gamma)\Lambda^2\right].
	\end{aligned}
\]
Thus, after moving all the terms containing $|A|^2$ to the left hand side and using the obvious inequality $0\leq (g-k)_+\leq 1$, we get 
\[
	\int_{\Omega_k}\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\gamma}|A|^2\varphi^2 \leq  (1+\alpha)\int_\Sigma(g-k)^2_+|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2+(C(n,\alpha)+C(n,\gamma))\Lambda^2\int_{\Omega_k}\varphi^2,
\]
where 
\[
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\gamma}\coloneqq (g-k)^2+(1+\alpha)\left(1-\frac{k}{g}\right)\left((1-\gamma)(1-g^2)-\frac{n-1}{n}-\gamma-\gamma g^2\right).
\]
By direct computation,
\[
	\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\gamma} = \left(1-\frac{k}{g}\right)\left(\frac{1+\alpha}{n}-kg-\alpha g^2-2(1+\alpha)\gamma\right).
\]
We can choose $\alpha=\alpha_n\coloneqq 1/(8n)$ and $\gamma=\gamma_n\coloneqq 1/(16n(1+\alpha_n))$ to obtain
\[
	\frac{1+\alpha}{n}-kg-\alpha g^2-2(1+\alpha)\gamma\geq \frac{1}{2n}
\]
since $k\in[0, 1/(4n)]$ and $0\leq g\leq 1$ by definition. Inequality \eqref{eq: Caccioppoli smooth A 1} then follows.

As for \eqref{eq: Caccioppoli smooth nabla 1}, recall by \Cref{lemma: diff ineq tilt} on the open set $\{g>0\}$ we have $\nabla_\Sigma g=\theta A(v,\cdot)+B(v,\cdot)$ for the unit vector $v=g^{-1}X$. Therefore, on $\Sigma\subset \mathcal{N}^+_{\rho_0}(P_R)$ 
\[
	|\nabla_{\Sigma}g|^2\leq 2\theta^2|A|^2+2|B|^2\leq 2|A|^2+2|\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M}|^2\leq 2|A|^2+2\Lambda^2,
\]
and \eqref{eq: Caccioppoli smooth nabla 1} then follows from \eqref{eq: Caccioppoli smooth A 1}.

Finally, apply \Cref{cor: cap} on a compact neighbourhood of $\operatorname{spt} \varphi$: there exist Lipschitz cutoff functions $\chi_j$, each supported away from $\mathcal{S}$, such that $0\leq \chi_j\leq 1$, $\chi_j\to 1$ locally on $\Sigma$, and $\int_{\Sigma}|\nabla_\Sigma\chi_j|^2\to 0$.
We can then apply the smooth inequality \eqref{eq: Caccioppoli smooth A 1} with test function $\chi_j\varphi$ to get
\[
	\begin{aligned}
		\frac{1}{2n} \int_{\{g>k\}} |A|^2 \left(1-\frac{k}{g}\right)\chi_j^2\varphi^2 \leq C(n) \int_\Sigma (g-k)_+^2|\nabla_\Sigma(\chi_j\varphi)|^2 + C(n)\Lambda^2\int_{\{g>k\}}\chi_j^2\varphi^2.
	\end{aligned}
\]
Since
\[
	|\nabla_\Sigma(\chi_j\varphi)|^2 \leq 2\chi_j^2|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2 + 2\varphi^2|\nabla_\Sigma\chi_j|^2,
\]
and  $0\leq (g-k)_+\leq 1$, the contribution of the second term is bounded by $\|\varphi\|_{L^\infty}^2 \int_{\Sigma}|\nabla_\Sigma\chi_j|^2$,
which tends to zero. All other terms pass to the limit by Fatou's lemma, the dominated convergence theorem, and the local area bound, cf. \Cref{rmk: area bounds}. Thus, letting $j\to\infty$, we obtain the asserted estimate with $|A|^2$ on the left-hand side. Then \eqref{eq: Caccioppoli smooth nabla 1} follows exactly as above.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{def: adapted neighbourhood}
	Let $\rho>0$. An \emph{adapted $\rho$-neighbourhood} of $P_R$ in $M$ consists of a relatively open neighbourhood $U\subset M$ of $P_{2R}$ and a smooth function $z_P:U\to \mathbb{R}$ with the following properties:
	\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
		\item $d_{\partial M}$ is smooth in $U$;
		\item $P\cap U=\{z_P=0\}$ and $\nabla^\mathrm{g} z_P$ is a positive multiple of $\nu_P$ on $P\cap U$;
		\item $\frac{1}{2}\leq|\nabla^\mathrm{g}z_P|\leq 2$ in $U$;
		\item $\langle \nabla^\mathrm{g}z_P, \mathbf{n}\rangle=0$ in $U\cap \partial M$.
        \item the flow $\Phi^Z_t$ of the vector field $Z=\frac{\nabla^\mathrm{g} z_P}{|\nabla^\mathrm{g}z_P|}$ defines a diffeomorphism $F_Z$ of $P_{2R}\times(-2\rho, 2\rho)$ onto a relatively open (compactly contained in $U$) neighbourhood of $P_{2R}$ in $M$ by
		\begin{equation}                           
		F_Z(y, t)\coloneqq \Phi_t^Z(y)\quad \text{ for $(y,t)\in P_{2R}\times(-2\rho, 2\rho)$.}
		\end{equation}
	\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- remark-20: body_md -->
\label{rmk: no gradient}
	The essential datum in an adapted neighbourhood is the vector field $Z$. One could instead prescribe a smooth unit vector field on a relatively open neighbourhood $U$ of $P_{2R}$, equal to $\nu_P$ on $P_{2R}$ and tangent to $\partial M$ along $\partial M\cap U$, whose flow satisfies condition~(v) of \Cref{def: adapted neighbourhood}. With the derivative bounds \eqref{eq: Z estimates}, the flow estimate \eqref{eq: derivative flow map}--\eqref{eq: estimate transversality}, and the ambient geometric bounds used below, the $\varepsilon$-regularity and sheeting arguments apply without requiring $Z$ to be a normalized gradient.
	
	In particular, the tilt and Caccioppoli estimates involve only the first two derivatives of $Z$, whereas deriving these bounds from $Z=\nabla^{\mathrm g}z_P/|\nabla^{\mathrm g}z_P|$ requires control of three derivatives of $z_P$. The function $z_P$ therefore provides a convenient construction, rather than an essential ingredient of the argument.
<!-- end remark-20 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lemma: adapted neighbourhood}
Let $P\subset M$ be as above and let $p\in\partial_0P$. Then there exists $0<\rho<\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{inj}_p(P)$ sufficiently small such that $P_\rho$ has an adapted $\rho$-neighbourhood $U$ in the sense of \Cref{def: adapted neighbourhood}.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Consider the smooth embedded relatively compact submanifold $\Gamma\coloneqq \partial_0P$ of $\partial M$. By \cite[Section 3.1]{Gray}, there exists $\varrho_0>0$ such that the normal exponential map $F_{\Gamma}$ of $\Gamma$ in $\partial M$ is a diffeomorphism of $\Gamma\times(-\varrho_0, \varrho_0)$ onto an open set $O_\Gamma$ in $\partial M$. Since $O_\Gamma$ has compact closure in $\partial M$, then, as mentioned in \Cref{sec: preliminaries}, after shrinking $\varrho_0$ if needed, the normal exponential map of $\partial M$ is a diffeomorphism of $O_\Gamma\times [0, \varrho_0)$ into a normal collar neighbourhood $U_\Gamma$ of $O_\Gamma$ in $M$. In particular, the composition of the two normal exponential maps gives a diffeomorphism $\Psi$ of $\Gamma\times (-\varrho_0, \varrho_0)\times [0, \varrho_0)$ onto $U_\Gamma\subset M$ by
\[
\Psi: (q, s, r)\mapsto F_{\partial M}\bigl(\operatorname{exp}^{\partial M}_q(s\nu_P(q)), r\bigr).
\]
Let $\widehat\Pi$ be the map $(q, s, r)\mapsto(q,r)$, and let $\Pi: P\cap U_\Gamma \to \Gamma\times[0,\varrho_0)$ be defined by $\Pi = \widehat\Pi\circ\Psi^{-1}$. 
Since $P$ meets $\partial M$ orthogonally along $\Gamma$, for all $q\in \Gamma$ we have $\Pi(q)=(q,0)$, and it is easy to check that $D\Pi_q|_{T_qP}$ is an isomorphism. By the implicit function theorem, there is $\varrho >0$ and an open set $U_\varrho\subset U_\Gamma$ such that for a neighbourhood $\Gamma_p$ of $p$ in $\Gamma$ we have a smooth function $\psi:\Gamma_p\times[0, \varrho)\to(-\varrho, \varrho)$ with the property that
\begin{equation}\label{eq: P graphical}
	P\cap U_\varrho=\{\Psi(q, \psi(q, r), r): q\in \Gamma_p, r\in[0, \varrho) \}, \quad \psi(q, 0)=0, \quad \partial_r\psi(q, 0)=0. 
\end{equation}
Define the function $z_P$ on $U_\varrho$ by $z_P(\Psi(q, s, r))\coloneqq s-\psi(q, r)$. We shall slightly abuse notation and write $z_P(q,s,r)=s-\psi(q,r)$.
Notice that on $U_\varrho \cap \partial M$ (corresponding to $\{r=0\}$) we have $\partial_r z_P=-\partial_r \psi(q, 0)=0$, so $\langle\nabla z_P, \mathbf{n}\rangle=0$ on  $U_\varrho \cap \partial M$. Moreover, $\partial_sz_P=1$ everywhere, so that $\mathrm{d}z_P\neq 0$. Since $z_P=0$ on $P\cap U_\varrho$ and $\mathrm{d}z_P\neq 0$, then $\nabla^\mathrm{g}z_P$ is normal to $P$ on $P\cap U_\varrho$. 
Also, by construction, $|\nabla^\mathrm{g}z_P|=1$ on $U_\varrho \cap \partial M$, so that, by shrinking $\varrho$ if necessary, we have $1/2\leq |\nabla^\mathrm{g}z_P|\leq 2$ on $U_\varrho$.

By the flowout theorem (see \cite[Theorem 9.20 and Theorem 9.34]{LeeSmooth}), there is $\rho\in(0, \varrho)$ such that (v) in \Cref{def: adapted neighbourhood} is satisfied with $U=U_\varrho$ and $R=\rho$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- remark-21: body_md -->
For the compactness argument in \Cref{subsec: top stratum}, it suffices to consider reference half-discs obtained by restricting $F_{\partial M}(\Gamma\times[0,\varrho))$ to a neighbourhood of a point of $\Gamma$. In the coordinates of the preceding proof, these are given by $s=0$, so that $\psi\equiv0$ and $z_P=s$.
	By choosing $T_p\Gamma$ appropriately, such reference half-discs realize every tangent half-hyperplane at $p$ orthogonal to $\partial M$. We nevertheless establish sheeting for arbitrary smooth two-sided reference half-discs meeting $\partial M$ orthogonally, since the argument applies in this greater generality.
<!-- end remark-21 -->
<!-- remark-22: body_md -->
\label{rmk: boundary preserved}
	Notice that, since $Z$ is tangential to $\partial M$ along $\partial M\cap U_1$ and does not vanish anywhere, then the flow preserves $\partial M$ and the interior of $M$, i.e. $F_Z\bigl(\partial_0P_{2\rho_1}\times(-2\rho_1, 2\rho_1)\bigr)=\mathcal{N}^Z_{2\rho_1}(P_{2\rho_1})\cap \partial M$.
<!-- end remark-22 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lemma: quantitative adapted neighbourhood}
	Let $P$, $\rho_1$, $U_1$, $z_P$ and $Z$ be chosen as above, and suppose that \eqref{eq: geometric bounds orthogonal} holds. There exists a constant $\delta_1=\delta_1(n, \Lambda, \rho_1)>0$ such that for all $\delta, R\in(0, \delta_1)$ the following estimates hold on $\mathcal{N}^Z_{2\delta}(P_{2R})$:
	\begin{equation}\label{eq: zP estimates}
		|\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}z_P|\leq C(n)\Lambda, \quad |\nabla^3_\mathrm{g}z_P|\leq C(n)\Lambda^2,
	\end{equation}
	\begin{equation}\label{eq: Z estimates}
		|\nabla_\mathrm{g}Z|\leq C(n)\Lambda, \quad |\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}Z|\leq C(n)\Lambda^2.
	\end{equation}
	Moreover, the flow map $F_Z$ restricted to $P_{2R}\times(-2\delta, 2\delta)$ is a diffeomorphism onto $\mathcal{N}^Z_{2\delta}(P_{2R})$ and it satisfies
	\begin{equation}\label{eq: derivative flow map}
		|DF_Z|+|D(F_Z^{-1})|\leq C(n).
	\end{equation}
	Finally, if $(y,t)\in P_{2R}\times(-2\delta, 2\delta)$ and $x=F_Z(y, t)$, then
	\begin{equation}\label{eq: estimate transversality}
		\begin{aligned}
			\sup_{v\in T_yP, |v|=1}\bigl|\langle Z(x), (D_yF_Z)_{(y, t)} v\rangle\bigr| \leq C(n) \bigl(\sup|\nabla^\mathrm{g}Z|\bigr) |t|\leq C(n)\Lambda |t|.
		\end{aligned}
	\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Recall the construction in the proof of \Cref{lemma: adapted neighbourhood}, and the definition of the map $\Psi:\partial_0P_{2\rho_1}\times(-2\rho_1, 2\rho_1)\times [0, 2\rho_1)\to U_1$ given by
\[
\Psi: (q, s, r)\mapsto F_{\partial M}\bigl(\operatorname{exp}^{\partial M}_q(s\nu_P(q)), r\bigr).
\]
Let $(\overline{\mathrm{g}}_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n+1}$ denote the pullback metric tensor $\Psi^*\mathrm{g}$ and let $\theta=\theta(n, \Lambda, \rho_1)\in(0,1/2)$ be a constant to be determined in the proof; then let $\rho_\theta\coloneqq 2\theta\rho_1$. By the estimates of \cite[Appendix A]{Li2021} and the Riccati equation, there exists a constant $c_0(n)$ such that, provided we choose $\theta>0$ small enough that $\rho_\theta\Lambda\leq c_0(n)$, then 
\begin{equation}\label{eq: estimates Fermi metric}
	\begin{aligned}
	\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{Euc}}\leq \overline{\mathrm{g}}\leq 2\mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{Euc}},\quad |\Gamma(\overline{\mathrm{g}})|\leq C(n)\Lambda,\quad |\partial\Gamma(\overline{\mathrm{g}})|\leq C(n)\Lambda^2
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
on $\partial_0P_{\rho_\theta}\times(-\rho_\theta, \rho_\theta)\times [0, \rho_\theta)$, where by $\Gamma(\overline{\mathrm{g}})$ we denote the Christoffel symbols of the metric $\overline{\mathrm{g}}$ and with $\partial \Gamma(\overline{\mathrm{g}})$ the collection of their partial derivatives with respect to the coordinate vector fields. Moreover, since $D\psi=0$ on $\partial_0P_{\rho_1}$, by shrinking $\theta$ if needed, we can assume $|D\psi|\leq 1$ on  $\partial_0P_{\rho_\theta}\times [0, \rho_\theta)$.
Therefore, using the graph representation in \eqref{eq: P graphical} together with the equation for the components of the second fundamental form of a graph, we obtain 
\begin{equation}\label{eq: D2psi}
	|D^2\psi|\leq C(n)\bigl(\textstyle \sup_{P_{2\rho_1}}|A_P|+|\Gamma(\overline{\mathrm{g}})|\bigr)\leq C(n)\Lambda
\end{equation}
on $\partial_0P_{\rho_\theta}\times [0, \rho_\theta)$.
                                                                                                                      
                                  
                                                                                                                     
               
Finally, differentiating the equation for the second fundamental form components in the tangential directions, we obtain 
\begin{equation}\label{eq: D3psi}
	|D^3\psi|\leq C(n)\bigl(\textstyle\sup_{P_{2\rho_1}}|\nabla_PA_P|+|\partial\Gamma(\overline{\mathrm{g}})| +\bigl(\sup_{P_{2\rho_1}}|A_P|+|\Gamma(\overline{\mathrm{g}})|+|D^2\psi|\bigr)^2\bigr)\leq C(n)\Lambda^2
\end{equation}
after shrinking $c_0(n)>0$ further, if necessary.

Recall in the proof of \Cref{lemma: adapted neighbourhood}, we defined the function $z_P$ by $z_P(\Psi(q, s, r))\coloneqq s-\psi(q, r)$. 
By differentiating the definition and using \eqref{eq: D2psi}--\eqref{eq: D3psi}, it is immediate to obtain the higher order estimates \eqref{eq: zP estimates} in $\mathcal{N}_{\rho_\theta}(P_{\rho_\theta})$.
Finally, by definition of $Z$, for every vector $X$, 
\begin{equation}\label{eq: derivative Z}
	\nabla^\mathrm{g}_XZ=|\nabla^\mathrm{g}z_P|^{-1}\left(\mathrm{Id}-Z\otimes Z\right)\left(\nabla^2_{\mathrm{g}}z_P(X, \cdot)\right)^\sharp.
\end{equation}
Therefore, \eqref{eq: Z estimates} follows from \eqref{eq: derivative Z} and \eqref{eq: zP estimates}.

By standard ODE theory, bounds on $|Z|, |\nabla^\mathrm{g}Z|, |\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}Z|$, together with \eqref{eq: geometric bounds orthogonal}, control the flow map $F_Z$ in $C^2$. In particular, on $P_{\rho_\theta}\times (-\rho_\theta, \rho_\theta)$, after shrinking $c_0(n)$ if needed, since $|t|\leq \rho_\theta$ and $\rho_\theta\Lambda\leq c_0(n)$, we obtain \eqref{eq: derivative flow map} and \eqref{eq: estimate transversality}.
Thus, we can let $\delta_1\coloneqq \frac{1}{2}\theta \rho_1$, which satisfies all the required properties.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
\label{def: adapted tilt}
 Let $\mtxcal{X}\coloneqq \mathrm{proj}^{T\Sigma}_\mathrm{g}(Z)$, $\tau=|\mtxcal{X}|_\mathrm{g}$, and $\vartheta\coloneqq \mathrm{g}(\nu, Z)$, where $Z$ is the vector field in \Cref{def: adapted neighbourhood}.
