 Then $\tau$ is a tilt function on $\Sigma$, with respect to $P$, adapted to $\partial M$. We will refer to it, for brevity, as the \emph{adapted tilt function}.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lemma: adapted tilt differential inequality}
Let $P, \delta_1, Z$ be as in \Cref{lemma: quantitative adapted neighbourhood}. Additionally, let $\Sigma$ be a smooth two-sided free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $M$ and assume that $\Sigma\subset \mathcal{N}^Z_\delta(P_R)$. Let $\nu$ be a choice of unit normal to $\Sigma$, $A$ its second fundamental form, and let $\tau, \mtxcal{X}, \vartheta$ be as in \Cref{def: adapted tilt}. Then $\tau^2+\vartheta^2=1$. Let $\mathcal{B}$ be the (0,2)-tensor on $\Sigma$ corresponding to $\nabla^{\mathrm{g}}Z|_{T\Sigma}$. Then the following hold.

First, on $\{\tau>0\}$, if $\mathcal{v}\coloneqq \mtxcal{X}/\tau$, then
\[
\nabla_\Sigma \tau=\vartheta A(\cdot,\mathcal{v})+\mathcal{B}(\cdot,\mathcal{v})
\]
and, for every $\gamma>0$,
\[
|\nabla_\Sigma \tau|^2 \leq (1+\gamma)(1-\tau^2)|A(\cdot,\mathcal{v})|^2 + C(\gamma) |\mathcal{B}|^2.
\]
Second, assuming that \eqref{eq: geometric bounds orthogonal} holds, then for every $\gamma>0$ on $\{\tau>0\}$
\[
\begin{aligned}
	\tau\Delta_\Sigma \tau
	&\geq (1-\gamma)|A|^2(1-\tau^2)-\left(\frac{n-1}{n}+\gamma\right)|A|^2-\gamma |A|^2\tau^2-C(n, \gamma)\Lambda^2.
\end{aligned}
\]
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
We shall follow the outline of the proof of \Cref{lemma: diff ineq tilt}, and highlight the analogies and differences. Notice that $|Z|_\mathrm{g}=1$ and $Z=\mtxcal{X}+\vartheta\nu$ along $\Sigma$, so that $\tau^2+\vartheta^2=1$.

Analogously to \Cref{lemma: diff ineq tilt}, let $\mathcal{B}$ be the (0,2)-tensor on $\Sigma$ corresponding to $\nabla^{\mathrm{g}}Z|_{T\Sigma}$, so that for $V,W\in T\Sigma$,  $\mathcal{B}(V, W)$ is defined by $\mathcal{B}(V, W)\coloneqq \langle\nabla^\mathrm{g}_V Z, W\rangle$.

Notice that for $V\in T\Sigma$
\[
	\nabla^\mathrm{g}_V\mtxcal{X} = \nabla^\mathrm{g}_VZ-(V\vartheta)\nu-\vartheta\nabla^\mathrm{g}_V\nu=\nabla^\mathrm{g}_VZ-(V\vartheta)\nu+\vartheta A(V, \cdot)^\sharp.
\]
If we take a tangential projection,
\[
	\nabla^\Sigma_V\mtxcal{X} = (\nabla^\mathrm{g}_VZ)^T+\vartheta A(V, \cdot)^\sharp.
\]
Therefore for $V,W\in T\Sigma$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq: adapted B+theta A}
	\mathrm{g}(\nabla^\Sigma_V\mtxcal{X}, W)=\mathrm{g}(\nabla^\mathrm{g}_VZ, W)+\vartheta A(V, W) = \mathcal{B}(V, W)+\vartheta A(V,W).
\end{equation}
By taking $W=\mtxcal{X}$ in this identity, we immediately obtain that
\[
\tau V\tau = \frac{1}{2}V(\tau^2)=\mathrm{g}(\nabla^\Sigma_V\mtxcal{X}, \mtxcal{X})=\mathcal{B}(V,\mtxcal{X})+\vartheta A(V, \mtxcal{X}).
\]
On $\{\tau >0\}$, writing $\mtxcal{X}=\tau \mathcal{v}$, this becomes $V\tau = \vartheta A(V, \mathcal{v})+\mathcal{B}(V, \mathcal{v})$, or, equivalently, $\nabla_\Sigma\tau=\vartheta A(\cdot, \mathcal{v})+\mathcal{B}(\cdot, \mathcal{v})$.
Taking norms, we obtain
\[
	|\nabla_\Sigma\mtxcal{X}|^2=\vartheta^2|A|^2+2\vartheta\langle A, \mathcal{B} \rangle +|\mathcal{B}|^2 = |A|^2(1-\tau^2)+2\vartheta\langle A, \mathcal{B} \rangle +|\mathcal{B}|^2.
\]
It follows from Young's inequality that for any fixed $\gamma>0$
\[
|\nabla_\Sigma \tau|^2 \leq (1+\gamma)\vartheta^2|A(\cdot, \mathcal{v})|^2 + C(\gamma) |\mathcal{B}(\cdot, \mathcal{v})|^2.
\]
Since $\vartheta^2=1-\tau^2$ and $|\mathcal{B}(\cdot, \mathcal{v})|\leq |\mathcal{B}|$, we obtain
\[
|\nabla_\Sigma \tau|^2 \leq (1+\gamma)(1-\tau^2)|A(\cdot, \mathcal{v})|^2 + C(\gamma) |\mathcal{B}|^2.
\]


Let $Y\in T\Sigma$ be a tangent vector field. 
Then we have the following Bochner identity (see \cite[Theorem 9.4.1]{Petersen})
\[
	\begin{aligned}
		\Delta_\Sigma\frac{|Y|^2}{2} &= |\nabla_\Sigma Y|^2+\langle Y, \nabla_\Sigma\operatorname{div}_\Sigma Y \rangle+ \langle Y, \operatorname{div}_\Sigma ((\nabla_\Sigma Y) - (\nabla_\Sigma Y)^*)\rangle+\operatorname{Ric}_\Sigma (Y,Y).\\
	                                                                                                                                                                       
	\end{aligned}
\]
As in the proof of \Cref{lemma: diff ineq tilt}, since $H=0$, by the contracted Gauss equation
\[
	\operatorname{Ric}_\Sigma (Y,Y) = \operatorname{Ric}_{\mathrm{g}}(Y,Y)-\operatorname{Rm}_\mathrm{g}(Y, \nu, Y, \nu)-|A(Y, \cdot)|^2.
\]
Now, letting $Y=\mtxcal{X}$ in the Bochner formula, we notice that by \eqref{eq: adapted B+theta A}, 
\[
	\operatorname{div}_\Sigma \mtxcal{X} = \operatorname{tr}_\Sigma(\mathcal{B}+\vartheta A)=\operatorname{tr}_\Sigma\mathcal{B} + \vartheta H = \operatorname{tr}_\Sigma\mathcal{B}
\]
since $H=0$ by minimality of $\Sigma$. Moreover, let $\mathcal K$ denote the skew-symmetric $(0,2)$-tensor on $\Sigma$ defined by $\mathcal{K}(V, W)=\langle\nabla^\Sigma_V\mtxcal{X}, W\rangle-\langle\nabla^\Sigma_W\mtxcal{X}, V\rangle$ for $V, W\in T\Sigma$, and notice that this term does not appear in the proof of \Cref{lemma: diff ineq tilt}, since in that situation $\nabla_\Sigma X$ is symmetric, because $X$ is a gradient vector field.

Since $\tau=|\mtxcal{X}|_{\mathrm{g}}$, we obtain 
\[
	\begin{aligned}
		\tau\Delta_\Sigma \tau+|\nabla_\Sigma \tau|^2 = \Delta_\Sigma \frac{\tau^2}{2} &= |\nabla_\Sigma \mtxcal{X}|^2 + \langle \mtxcal{X}, \nabla_\Sigma \operatorname{tr}_\Sigma \mathcal{B}\rangle + \langle \mtxcal{X}, \operatorname{div}_\Sigma \mathcal K\rangle+\operatorname{Ric}_\Sigma(\mtxcal{X}, \mtxcal{X})\\
		&= |A|^2(1-\tau^2)+2\vartheta\langle A, \mathcal{B} \rangle +|\mathcal{B}|^2\\
		&\quad +\langle \mtxcal{X}, \nabla_\Sigma \operatorname{tr}_\Sigma \mathcal{B}\rangle + \langle \mtxcal{X}, \operatorname{div}_\Sigma \mathcal K\rangle\\
		& \qquad + \operatorname{Ric}_{\mathrm{g}}(\mtxcal{X},\mtxcal{X})-\operatorname{Rm}_\mathrm{g}(\mtxcal{X}, \nu, \mtxcal{X}, \nu)-|A( \cdot, \mtxcal{X})|^2,
	\end{aligned}
\]
so that
\begin{equation}\label{eq: Bochner adapted}
	\begin{aligned}
		\tau\Delta_\Sigma\tau &=  |A|^2(1-\tau^2)-|A(\mtxcal{X}, \cdot)|^2-|\nabla_\Sigma \tau|^2 + 2\vartheta\langle A, \mathcal{B} \rangle +|\mathcal{B}|^2\\
		&\quad +\langle \mtxcal{X}, \nabla_\Sigma \operatorname{tr}_\Sigma \mathcal{B}\rangle + \langle \mtxcal{X}, \operatorname{div}_\Sigma \mathcal K\rangle\\
		& \qquad + \operatorname{Ric}_{\mathrm{g}}(\mtxcal{X},\mtxcal{X})-\operatorname{Rm}_\mathrm{g}(\mtxcal{X}, \nu, \mtxcal{X}, \nu),
	\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Because $H=0$, $A$ is trace-free. Hence, for every unit vector $v$,
$|A(v,\cdot)|^2\leq \frac{n-1}{n}|A|^2$, and 
\[
	|\nabla_\Sigma \tau|^2 \leq (1+\gamma)(1-\tau^2)\left(\frac{n-1}{n}\right)|A|^2 + C(\gamma) |\mathcal{B}|^2.
\]
The same computations as in the proof of \Cref{lemma: diff ineq tilt} also show that 
\[
	|A(\cdot, \mtxcal{X})|^2+|\nabla_\Sigma \tau|^2\leq \left(\frac{n-1}{n}+\gamma\right)|A|^2+C(\gamma)|\mathcal{B}|^2
\]
and
\[
	2|\vartheta\langle A,\mathcal{B}\rangle| \leq \gamma |A|^2\vartheta^2+C(\gamma) |\mathcal{B}|^2 = \gamma |A|^2(1-\tau^2)+C(\gamma) |\mathcal{B}|^2.
\]
For the remaining terms in \eqref{eq: Bochner adapted}, similarly to \Cref{lemma: diff ineq tilt}, we have 
\[
	|\nabla_\Sigma\operatorname{tr}_\Sigma\mathcal{B}|+|\operatorname{div}_\Sigma\mathcal{K}|\leq C(n)(|\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}Z|+|A||\nabla^\mathrm{g}Z|).
\]
The bound in terms of $\Lambda$ then follows from \eqref{eq: geometric bounds orthogonal} since \[|\operatorname{Ric}_{\mathrm{g}}(\mtxcal{X},\mtxcal{X})-\operatorname{Rm}_\mathrm{g}(\mtxcal{X}, \nu, \mtxcal{X}, \nu)|\leq C(n)|\operatorname{Rm}_\mathrm{g}|\tau^2.\]
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lemma: partial tau boundary}
	Let $\eta$ be the inward unit conormal to $\partial \Sigma$ in $\Sigma$.
	Along $\partial \Sigma\cap \{\tau>0\}$ we have $|\partial_\eta \tau|\leq |\mathrm{I\!I}_{\partial M}|+|\nabla^\mathrm{g}Z|$ pointwise.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Let us differentiate the function $\vartheta$ in the direction of the unit conormal $\eta$ to $\partial \Sigma$ along $\partial \Sigma\cap\{\tau>0\}$. Notice that $\nabla^\mathrm{g}_\eta\nu\in T\Sigma$, so that
\[
\partial_\eta \vartheta = \mathrm{g}(\nabla^\mathrm{g}_\eta\nu, Z)+\mathrm{g}(\nu, \nabla^\mathrm{g}_\eta Z) = \mathrm{g}(\nabla^\mathrm{g}_\eta\nu, \mtxcal{X})+\mathrm{g}(\nu, \nabla^\mathrm{g}_\eta Z)=-A(\eta,\mtxcal{X})+\mathrm{g}(\nu, \nabla^\mathrm{g}_\eta Z).
\]
By definition, $Z$ is tangent to $\partial M$ in $\partial M\cap \mathcal{N}^Z_\delta(P_R)$. Therefore, along $\partial \Sigma \cap\{\tau>0\} \subset \partial M\cap \mathcal{N}^Z_\delta(P_R)$, we have
\[
	\mathrm{g}(\mtxcal{X}, \mathbf{n})=\mathrm{g}(Z-\vartheta\nu, \mathbf{n}) = \mathrm{g}(Z, \mathbf{n})-\vartheta \mathrm{g}(\nu, \mathbf{n})=0,
\]
and $\mtxcal{X}$ is also tangent to $\partial M$ along $\partial \Sigma$.
Hence, by symmetry of the second fundamental form and the free-boundary condition
\[
	A(\eta, \mtxcal{X})=A(\mtxcal{X}, \eta)=-\mathrm{g}(\nabla^\mathrm{g}_{\mtxcal{X}}\nu, \eta)=-\mathrm{g}(\nabla^\mathrm{g}_{\mtxcal{X}}\nu, \mathbf{n})=-\mathrm{I\!I}_{\partial M}(\mtxcal{X}, \nu)
\]
and
\[
	\partial_\eta \vartheta = \mathrm{I\!I}_{\partial M}(\mtxcal{X}, \nu)+\mathrm{g}(\nu, \nabla^\mathrm{g}_\eta Z).
\]
Clearly, $|\mathrm{I\!I}_{\partial M}(\mtxcal{X}, \nu)|\leq |\mathrm{I\!I}_{\partial M}||\mtxcal{X}||\nu|=\tau |\mathrm{I\!I}_{\partial M}|$. Moreover, since $|Z|_\mathrm{g}\equiv 1$, 
\[
	|\mathrm{g}(\nu, \nabla^\mathrm{g}_\eta Z)|=|\mathrm{g}(\nu-\vartheta Z, \nabla^\mathrm{g}_\eta Z)|\leq |\nu-\vartheta Z||\eta||\nabla^\mathrm{g}Z|=\tau |\nabla^\mathrm{g}Z|.
\]
Finally, 
\[
	\tau |\partial_\eta \tau|=|\vartheta \partial_\eta \vartheta|\leq |\vartheta| \tau(|\mathrm{I\!I}_{\partial M}|+|\nabla^\mathrm{g}Z|)\leq \tau(|\mathrm{I\!I}_{\partial M}|+|\nabla^\mathrm{g}Z|)
\]
since $|\vartheta|\leq 1$. This concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lemma: Caccioppoli FB singular}
Let $\Sigma$ be a stable \emph{singular} free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $M$ and assume that  $\overline{\Sigma}\subset \mathcal{N}^Z_\delta(P_R)$. Assume that \eqref{eq: geometric bounds orthogonal} holds. Then there exist a positive dimensional constant $C(n)>0$ and a constant $ K_{\mathrm{Cac}}=K_{\mathrm{Cac}}(n,\Lambda)>0$ depending on $n$ and $\Lambda$ such that for every $k\in[0,1/(4n)]$ and every $\varphi\in C_c^{0,1}(\mathcal{N}^Z_{\rho_1}(P_R))$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq: Caccioppoli smooth A 2}
	\begin{aligned}
		\frac{1}{2n}\int_{\{\tau>k\}}
		|A|^2
		\left(1-\frac{k}{\tau}\right)\varphi^2
		&\leq
		C(n)\left(\int_\Sigma (\tau-k)_+^2|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2
		+K_\mathrm{Cac}^2\int_{\{\tau>k\}}\varphi^2\right).
	\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq: Caccioppoli smooth nabla 2}
	\begin{aligned}
		\frac{1}{2n}\int_{\{\tau>k\}}
		|\nabla_\Sigma \tau|^2
		\left(1-\frac{k}{\tau}\right)\varphi^2
		&\leq
		C(n)\left(\int_\Sigma (\tau-k)_+^2|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2
		+K_{\mathrm{Cac}}^2\int_{\{\tau>k\}}\varphi^2\right).
	\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
We shall first prove \eqref{eq: Caccioppoli smooth A 2} and \eqref{eq: Caccioppoli smooth nabla 2} for test functions $\varphi\in C^{0,1}_c(\Sigma)$, and then extend to compactly supported ambient Lipschitz functions by an excision argument, similar to the one in \Cref{lemma: trace inequality}.

Let $\Omega_k\coloneqq\{\tau>k\}$, and apply \Cref{lemma: stability absorbed 1} to $\zeta=(\tau-k)_+\varphi$ to obtain 
\begin{equation}\label{eq: Caccioppoli stability 1}
	\int_{\Sigma}|A|^2(\tau-k)_+^2\varphi^2\leq (1+\alpha)\int_\Sigma|\nabla_\Sigma((\tau-k)_+\varphi)|^2+C(n,\alpha)\Lambda^2 \int_\Sigma (\tau-k)^2_+\varphi^2.
\end{equation}
Let $J\coloneqq  \int_\Sigma|\nabla_\Sigma((\tau-k)_+\varphi)|^2$ and integrate by parts to get 
\begin{equation}\label{eq: J by parts}
\int_\Sigma |\nabla_\Sigma((\tau-k)_+\varphi)|^2 = -\int_{\Omega_k}(\tau-k)\Delta_\Sigma \tau\,\varphi^2 + \int_\Sigma (\tau-k)_+^2|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2-\int_{\partial\Sigma \cap \Omega_k}(\tau-k)_+\partial_\eta\tau \varphi^2. 
\end{equation}
By \Cref{lemma: adapted tilt differential inequality}, on $\Omega_k$ we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq: use tilt differential inequality}
	-(\tau-k)\Delta_\Sigma\tau\leq -\left(1-\frac{k}{\tau}\right)\left[\left((1-\gamma)(1-\tau^2)-\frac{n-1}{n}-\gamma-\gamma\tau^2\right)|A|^2-C(n,\gamma)\Lambda^2\right].
\end{equation}
Then \eqref{eq: Caccioppoli stability 1} becomes
\[
	\begin{aligned}
		\int_{\Omega_k}&\left(1-\frac{k}{\tau}\right)\left(\tau(\tau-k)+(1+\alpha)\left(\frac{1}{n}-\tau^2-2\gamma\right)\right)|A|^2\varphi^2\\
		&\leq (1+\alpha)\int_\Sigma (\tau-k)^2_+|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2+C(n,\alpha)\Lambda^2 \int_\Sigma(\tau-k)^2_+\varphi^2+(1+\alpha)C(n,\gamma)\Lambda^2\int_{\Omega_k}\varphi^2\\
		&\quad -(1+\alpha)\int_{\partial\Sigma \cap \Omega_k}(\tau-k)_+\partial_\eta\tau \varphi^2.
	\end{aligned}
\]
The same choices $\alpha=\alpha_n, \gamma=\gamma_n$ (depending on $n$ only) as in \Cref{lemma: Caccioppoli inequality 1} make the coefficient in front of $(1-k/\tau)|A|^2\varphi^2$ bounded below by $1/(2n)+1/(8n^2)>1/(2n)$. Also, noticing that $0\leq (\tau-k)_+\leq 1$ and $(\tau-k)_+=0$ outside $\Omega_k$, we can write the previous inequality as 
\[
	\begin{aligned}
		\frac{1}{2n}\int_{\Omega_k}\left(1-\frac{k}{\tau}\right)|A|^2\varphi^2 &\leq C(n)\int_\Sigma (\tau-k)^2_+|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2+C(n,\Lambda)\int_{\Omega_k}\varphi^2\\
		&\quad-C(n)\int_{\partial\Sigma \cap \Omega_k}(\tau-k)_+\partial_\eta\tau \varphi^2.
	\end{aligned}
\] 
Let us now deal with the boundary term. Let $B_k\coloneqq \int_{\partial\Sigma \cap \Omega_k}(\tau-k)_+\partial_\eta\tau \varphi^2$. By \Cref{lemma: partial tau boundary},
\[
	|B_k|\leq \left(\sup_{U_1}|\mathrm{I\!I}_{\partial M}|+\sup_{\mathcal{N}^Z_\delta(P_R)}|\nabla^\mathrm{g}Z|\right) \int_{\partial \Sigma}(\tau-k)_+\varphi^2\leq C(n ,\Lambda)\int_{\partial \Sigma}(\tau-k)_+\varphi^2
\]
where the second inequality follows from \Cref{lemma: quantitative adapted neighbourhood} and \eqref{eq: geometric bounds orthogonal}. 
By \Cref{lemma: trace inequality}, 
\[
	\int_{\partial \Sigma}(\tau-k)_+\varphi^2\leq \int_{\Omega_k}\varphi^2|\nabla_\Sigma\tau|+2\int_\Sigma(\tau-k)_+\varphi|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|+C_{\mathrm{tr}}(n, \Lambda)\int_\Sigma(\tau-k)_+\varphi^2.
\]
For every $\lambda>0$, by the weighted Young inequality
\[
	\begin{aligned}
		C(n, \Lambda)\left( \int_{\Omega_k}\varphi^2|\nabla_\Sigma\tau|+2\int_\Sigma(\tau-k)_+\varphi|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|\right) &\leq \lambda\int_{\Omega_k}\varphi^2|\nabla_\Sigma\tau|^2+\int_\Sigma(\tau-k)^2_+|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2\\
		&\quad +C(\lambda) C(n, \Lambda)\int_{\Omega_k}\varphi^2.
	\end{aligned}
\]
Similarly, since $(\tau-k)_+\leq \1_{\Omega_k}$, we can write $\int_\Sigma(\tau-k)_+\varphi^2\leq \int_{\Omega_k}\varphi^2$.
Thus,
\[
	\begin{aligned}
		|B_k| &\leq \lambda\int_{\Omega_k}\varphi^2|\nabla_\Sigma\tau|^2 +\int_{\Sigma}(\tau-k)^2_+|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2+C(\lambda) C(n, \Lambda)\int_{\Omega_k}\varphi^2.
	\end{aligned}	 
\]

Since $\varphi\nabla_\Sigma(\tau-k)_+=\nabla_\Sigma((\tau-k)_+\varphi)-(\tau-k)_+\nabla_\Sigma \varphi$, we have the obvious inequality $\int \varphi^2|\nabla_\Sigma(\tau-k)_+|^2\leq 2J+2\int (\tau-k)^2_+|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2$, so that
\begin{equation}\label{eq: absorption 1}
	|B_k|\leq 2\lambda J+ C(n,\lambda) \mathcal{R}_k
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{R}_k\coloneqq \int (\tau-k)^2_+|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2+C( n, \Lambda)\int_{\Omega_k}\varphi^2$.

Recall that by \eqref{eq: J by parts} and \eqref{eq: use tilt differential inequality}, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq: absorption 2}
J\leq  C(n)\int_{\Omega_k}\left(1-\frac{k}{\tau}\right)|A|^2\varphi^2 +  C(n)\mathcal{R}_k+|B_k|. 
\end{equation}
Therefore, by combining \eqref{eq: absorption 1} and \eqref{eq: absorption 2}, we obtain 
\[
	|B_k|\leq 2\lambda\left(C(n)\int_{\Omega_k}\left(1-\frac{k}{\tau}\right)|A|^2\varphi^2 +  C(n)\mathcal{R}_k+|B_k|\right)+C(n,\lambda)\mathcal{R}_k,
\]
i.e.
\begin{equation}\label{eq: absorption}
	|B_k|\leq \frac{2\lambda C(n)}{1-2\lambda} \int_{\Omega_k}\left(1-\frac{k}{\tau}\right)|A|^2\varphi^2 +\frac{2\lambda C(n)+ C(n, \lambda)}{1-2\lambda}\mathcal{R}_k.
\end{equation}

By choosing $\lambda>0$ small enough (depending on $n$ only) that $\frac{2\lambda C(n)}{1-2\lambda}<\frac{1}{8n^2}$, we can reabsorb  the multiple of $\int_{\Omega_k}\left(1-\frac{k}{\tau}\right)|A|^2\varphi^2 $ coming from \eqref{eq: absorption} into the left hand side and obtain the desired inequality.

Finally, for an ambient test function $\varphi$, first apply the inequality to $\chi_j\varphi$, where $\chi_j$ are the cutoff functions of \Cref{cor: cap}, chosen to equal one in the limit on $\operatorname{spt}\varphi\setminus\mathcal S$, then take the limit as in the proof of \Cref{lemma: Caccioppoli inequality 1}.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop: boundary titl height estimate}
Let $M, P, \rho_0, R_0$ be as in the statement of \Cref{thm: boundary eps reg}, and let $\delta\in(0, \rho_0), R\in(0, R_0)$. Suppose $\Sigma$ is a singular properly immersed free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $M$ such that $\overline{\Sigma}\subset \mathcal{N}^+_\delta(P_R)$. Then, there exists $C(n, \Lambda) > 0$ such that for $0<2r<R$ the following estimate holds 
\begin{equation} \label{eq: tilt-height excess estimate}
    \frac{1}{r^n} \int_{\Sigma \cap C_r} g^2 = \frac{1}{r^n} \int_{\Sigma \cap C_r} \vert \nabla_\Sigma d_{\partial M} \vert^2 \leq \frac{C(n, \Lambda)}{r^{n + 2}} \int_{\Sigma \cap C_{2r}} \left(d_{\partial M}^2 + r^2 d_{\partial M}\right),
\end{equation}
where $g$ is the tilt function in \Cref{def: tilt}.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Consider the vector field $X(x) = d_{\partial M}(x) \varphi^2(x) \nabla^\mathrm{g} d_{\partial M}(x)$, where $\varphi \in C_c^1(C_{2r})$ is a smooth cut-off taken to be identically $1$ on $C_r$ and satisfying $\vert \nabla \varphi \vert \leq C/r$ (for some absolute constant $C$). In particular, $X(x) \equiv 0$ on $\partial M$ so that we can test the first variation with it. 
In particular, by free-boundary stationarity
\[
	0=\int_\Sigma \operatorname{div}_\Sigma X=\int_\Sigma \varphi^2 g^2+2\varphi d_{\partial M}\langle\nabla_\Sigma \varphi, \nabla_\Sigma d_{\partial M}\rangle+\varphi^2 d_{\partial M} \operatorname{tr}_\Sigma\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}d_{\partial M},
\]
since the mean curvature of $\Sigma$ is assumed to vanish in $\Sigma \setminus \partial \Sigma$ by \Cref{def: fb smooth immersed min hyp}. Hence, 
\[
\begin{aligned}
	\int_{\Sigma \cap C_r}g^2 \leq \int_\Sigma \varphi^2 g^2\leq 2\int_\Sigma|\varphi \nabla_\Sigma d_{\partial M}| |d_{\partial M}\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|+\sqrt{n}\int_\Sigma \varphi^2d_{\partial M}|\nabla_\mathrm{g}^2d_{\partial M}|
\end{aligned}
\]
from which \eqref{eq: tilt-height excess estimate} follows by Young's inequality and \eqref{eq: geometric control on neighbourhood}.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop: orthogonal tilt height estimate}
Let $M, P, \rho_1, \delta_1, R_1$ be as in the statement of \Cref{thm: orthogonal eps reg}. Let $z_P$ and $Z$ be as in \Cref{def: adapted neighbourhood}, and let $\delta\in(0, \delta_1), R\in(0, R_1)$. Suppose $\Sigma$ is a  singular properly immersed free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $M$ such that $\overline{\Sigma}\subset \mathcal{N}^Z_\delta(P_R)$. Then, there exists $C(n, \Lambda) > 0$ such that for $0<2r<R$ the following estimate holds 
\begin{equation} \label{eq: orthogonal tilt height estimate}
    \frac{1}{r^n} \int_{\Sigma\cap\mathcal C^Z_r} \tau^2 \leq \frac{C(n, \Lambda)}{r^{n+2}} \int_{\Sigma\cap\mathcal C^Z_{2r}} \left(z_P^2+r^2|z_P|\right),
\end{equation}
where $\tau$ is the adapted tilt function in \Cref{def: adapted tilt}.\\
Moreover, since $ |\nabla_\Sigma z_P|^2=|\nabla^\mathrm{g} z_P|^2\tau^2,$ the same estimate holds, up to increasing the constant $C$, with $|\nabla_\Sigma z_P|^2$ in place of $\tau^2$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Let $W\coloneqq |\nabla^\mathrm{g}z_P|^{-1}Z$ and notice that $1/2\leq |W|_{\mathrm{g}}\leq 2$ by \Cref{def: adapted neighbourhood}.
For $0< 2r\leq R$ choose a cutoff $\chi$ on $P_R$ which is compactly supported on $P_{2r}$ and such that $0\leq\chi\leq1,$ and $ \chi\equiv 1$ on $P_r,$ as well as $|\nabla_{ P}\chi| \leq C/r,$ where $C$ is an absolute constant. Then pick a compactly supported vertical cutoff $\xi\geq \1_{[-\delta, \delta]}$ supported in $(-2\delta, 2\delta)$ and let $\varphi=(\xi\circ \mathcal{t}_Z)(\chi \circ \pi_Z)$, so that $\varphi\equiv 1 $ on $\mathcal C^Z_r$ as well as $\operatorname{spt}(\varphi)\cap\Sigma \subset\mathcal C^Z_{2r}$. Also, $\varphi$ satisfies $|\nabla\varphi|\leq C/r,$ where $C$ depends on $n, \Lambda, \rho_1$ only.

Consider then the vector field $ Y=\varphi^2z_PW.$ Since $W$ is tangent to $\partial M$ along $\partial M\cap \mathcal{N}^Z_\delta(P_R)$, then $Y$ is an admissible vector field for free-boundary stationarity and $\int_\Sigma \operatorname{div}_\Sigma Y = 0.$ 
Notice that 
\begin{equation}
	\label{eq: adapted tilt identity}
	\langle\nabla_\Sigma z_P,W\rangle = \left\langle |\nabla^\mathrm{g} z_P|Z^T, \frac{Z}{|\nabla^\mathrm{g} z_P|} \right\rangle = |Z^T|^2 = \tau^2.
\end{equation}
Therefore, 
\[
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{div}_\Sigma Y =\operatorname{div}_\Sigma(\varphi^2z_PW) &= \varphi^2\langle\nabla_\Sigma z_P,W\rangle +2\varphi z_P \langle\nabla_\Sigma\varphi,W\rangle +\varphi^2z_P\,\operatorname{div}_\Sigma W
\\ &=\varphi^2\tau^2+2\varphi z_P|\nabla^\mathrm{g}z_P|^{-1}\langle\nabla_\Sigma \varphi, Z^T\rangle+ \varphi^2z_P\operatorname{div}_\Sigma W.
\end{aligned}
\] 
Notice that
\[
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{div}_\Sigma W &= |\nabla^\mathrm{g}z_P|^{-1}\operatorname{div}_\Sigma Z-\left\langle Z, |\nabla^\mathrm{g}z_P|^{-2}\nabla_\Sigma |\nabla^\mathrm{g}z_P|\right\rangle   
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         
\end{aligned}
\]
so that $|\operatorname{div}_\Sigma W|\leq C(n)\bigl(|\nabla^\mathrm{g}Z|+|\nabla_\mathrm{g}^2z_P|\bigr)\leq C(n)\Lambda$ by \Cref{def: adapted neighbourhood} and \Cref{lemma: quantitative adapted neighbourhood}.
Combining all of the above, we obtain
\begin{align}   \label{eq: preliminary tilt height estimate}
    \int_\Sigma\varphi^2\tau^2
    &\leq 4 \int_\Sigma \varphi|z_P|\,|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|\,\tau + C(n)\Lambda \int_\Sigma \varphi^2|z_P|.
\end{align}
By Young's inequality, $ 4 \varphi|z_P|\,|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|\,\tau \leq \frac{1}{2}\varphi^2\tau^2 + 8z_P^2|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2.$ After absorbing the first term into the left-hand side, it follows that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: caccioppoli adapted tilt}
    \int_\Sigma\varphi^2\tau^2 \leq 16\int_\Sigma z_P^2|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2 + C(n)\Lambda\int_\Sigma \varphi^2|z_P|.
\end{equation}
Using properties of $\varphi$, we conclude that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: unscaled orthogonal tilt height estimate}
    \int_{\Sigma\cap\mathcal C^Z_r}
    \tau^2 \leq \frac{C}{r^2} \int_{\Sigma\cap\mathcal C^Z_{2r}} z_P^2 + C(n, \Lambda) \int_{\Sigma\cap\mathcal C^Z_{2r}} |z_P|.
\end{equation}
Dividing by $r^n$ proves
\eqref{eq: orthogonal tilt height estimate}.

Finally, $|\nabla_\Sigma z_P|^2 = |(\nabla^\mathrm{g} z_P)^T|^2 = |\nabla^\mathrm{g} z_P|^2|Z^T|^2 = |\nabla^\mathrm{g} z_P|^2\tau^2.$ Thus, $ (1/4)\tau^2 \leq |\nabla_\Sigma z_P|^2 \leq 4\tau^2,$ which proves the corresponding estimate for $|\nabla_\Sigma z_P|^2$.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- remark-23: body_md -->
The height--tilt estimate also admits a formulation using only $Z$ described in \Cref{rmk: no gradient}, right after \Cref{def: adapted neighbourhood}. In that
	formulation, use the flow time $\mathcal t_Z$ as height. Since
	$Z(\mathcal t_Z)=1$ and $\nabla^{\mathrm g}\mathcal t_Z=Z$ on $P$, the derivative and flow estimates give
	\[
	|\nabla^{\mathrm g}\mathcal t_Z-Z|
	\leq C(n)\Lambda|\mathcal t_Z|.
	\]
	Testing free-boundary stationarity with $\varphi^2\mathcal t_ZZ$ therefore yields \eqref{eq: orthogonal tilt height estimate} with $\mathcal t_Z$ in place of $z_P$: the error in replacing $\langle\nabla_\Sigma\mathcal t_Z,Z\rangle$ by $\tau^2$ is bounded by $C(n)\Lambda|\mathcal t_Z|\tau$, and hence by the linear height term already present in the estimate.
<!-- end remark-23 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lemma: first variation trace}
	Let $\Sigma$ be a singular properly embedded free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $M$ and let $V=|\Sigma|$ be its associated multiplicity-one varifold. Then
	\begin{equation}\label{eq: first variation singular FB}
		\delta V(X)=-\int_{\partial \Sigma}\mathrm{g}(X, \eta)\, d\mathcal{H}^{n-1} \quad \text{for all } X\in \mathfrak X(M),
	\end{equation}
	where $\eta$ denotes the inner unit conormal of $\partial \Sigma$ in $\Sigma$. Hence, for all $K\Subset M$, if $K\Subset\operatorname{int} K'\Subset M$, then 
	\begin{equation}\label{eq: locally finite deltaV}
		\sup\left\{|\delta V(X)|: X\in \mathfrak X(M), |X|_{\mathrm{g}}\leq 1, \operatorname{spt} X\subset K\right\}\leq C(K)\mathcal{H}^{n}(\Sigma \cap K'),
	\end{equation}
	i.e. the integral varifold $|\Sigma|$ has locally bounded first variation.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Notice that for all vector fields $X$ supported away from the singular set $\mathcal{S}$, \eqref{eq: first variation singular FB} holds because of \Cref{def: fb smooth immersed min hyp}. On the other hand, for a general $X\in \mathfrak{X}(M)$, if we let $\{\chi_j\}$ be the sequence of test functions in \Cref{cor: cap}, then $X_j\coloneqq \chi_j X$ is supported away from the singular set, and the same excision argument as in the proof of \Cref{lemma: trace inequality} proves the general result.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- remark-24: body_md -->
Notice that our proof crucially uses the property that $V$ is induced by a singular properly embedded free-boundary minimal hypersurface. On the other hand, \eqref{eq: locally finite deltaV} holds in general for free-boundary stationary varifolds, albeit with a more involved proof. The proof under the assumption of integrality is in \cite[Proposition 3.2]{Edelen2020}, and in full generality in \cite[Theorem 1.1]{Masi2020}.
<!-- end remark-24 -->
<!-- remark-25: body_md -->
\label{rmk: extension to limits}
	The conclusions of  \Cref{prop: compactness stationary FB}, \Cref{lemma: density bound}, \Cref{prop: existence VarTan}, and \Cref{prop: stratification} also hold for varifold limits of sequences of singular properly embedded free-boundary minimal hypersurfaces in $M$ with locally bounded mass. (This will be needed in \Cref{subsec: top stratum} and \Cref{subsec: slicing argument}.)
<!-- end remark-25 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop: compactness stationary FB}
	Let $(\Sigma_k)$ be a sequence of singular properly embedded free-boundary minimal hypersurfaces in $M$ and, for each $k$, let $V_k=|\Sigma_k|$ be the associated multiplicity-one varifold. Assume that for each $K\Subset M$
	\begin{equation}
		\sup_k\|V_k\|(K)\leq C(K)<+\infty.
	\end{equation}
	Then, up to a subsequence (not relabelled), 
	there is a free-boundary stationary integral varifold $V_\infty$ on $M$ such that 
	\begin{equation}
		V_k \to V_\infty \quad\text{in the sense of varifolds.}
	\end{equation}
	In particular, $\delta V_k \to \delta V_\infty$ and the limit has locally bounded first variation. Moreover, 
	\begin{equation}\label{eq: Hausdorff convergence}
		\operatorname{spt} \|V_k\| \to \operatorname{spt} \|V_\infty\|
	\end{equation}
	locally in Hausdorff distance.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
The first variation hypothesis in \cite[6.4]{Allard1972} is fulfilled, in view of \Cref{lemma: first variation trace}; this yields the subsequential limit $V_\infty$. The only conclusion that needs justification is \eqref{eq: Hausdorff convergence}, which follows from the standard proof for stationary integral varifolds (as remarked e.g. in \cite[Remark 10]{SchoenSimon81}) once the following lemma is established.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lemma: density bound}
	Let $\Sigma$ be a singular properly embedded free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $M$ and let $V=|\Sigma|$ be the associated multiplicity-one varifold in $M$. Then for each compact $K\subset M$ there exist constants $c_K>0, C_K, r_K>0$ such that 
	\begin{equation}\label{eq: density bound}
		c_Kr^n\leq \|V\|(B_r(y))\leq C_K r^n
	\end{equation}
	for all $y\in\operatorname{spt} \|V\|\cap K$, $0<r<r_K$.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Let $K\subset M$ be compact and pick $r_K>0$ small enough that 
\begin{itemize}
	\item if $K\subset M\setminus \partial M$, $2r_K \leq \inf_{x\in K}\operatorname{inj}_x(M)$;
	\item if $K\cap \partial M\neq \emptyset$, $F_{\partial M}$ is a diffeomorphism of $(K\cap \partial M)\times [0, 2r_K)$ onto the collar neighbourhood $\mathcal{N}^+_{2r_K}(K\cap \partial M)$ and $2 r_K\leq \inf_{x\in K\setminus \mathcal{N}^+_{2r_K}(K\cap \partial M)}\operatorname{inj}_x(M)$.
\end{itemize}
Then notice that by \cite[\S 17]{Simon1983} and \cite[Theorem 3.4]{Guang2020} there exist constants $\Lambda^\mathrm{int}_K, \Lambda^\mathrm{bd}_K$ depending on $\sup_K|\operatorname{Rm}_\mathrm{g}|$ and $\sup_{\mathcal{N}^+_{2r_K}(K\cap \partial M)}|\nabla^2_\mathrm{g} d_{\partial M}|$ such that for all $y\in K$
\begin{equation}\label{eq: interior monotonicity}
e^{\Lambda^\mathrm{int}_K\sigma}\frac{\|V\|(B_\sigma(y))}{\sigma^n}\leq e^{\Lambda^\mathrm{int}_K\rho}\frac{\|V\|(B_\rho(y))}{\rho^n}\quad\text{whenever }B_\rho(y)\cap \partial M=\emptyset, 0<\sigma<\rho<r_K,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq: boundary monotonicity}
e^{\Lambda^\mathrm{bd}_K\sigma}\frac{\|V\|(B_\sigma(y))}{\sigma^n}\leq e^{\Lambda^\mathrm{bd}_K\rho}\frac{\|V\|(B_\rho(y))}{\rho^n}\quad\text{whenever }y\in K\cap \partial M, 0<\sigma<\rho<r_K.
\end{equation}

For simplicity we shall prove the density ratio lower bound in \eqref{eq: density bound}. The proof of the upper bound follows a similar strategy.
Choose $c_K=16^{-n}\omega_n e^{-2\max\{\Lambda^\mathrm{int}_K, \Lambda^\mathrm{bd}_K\}r_K}>0$, and consider first a regular point $y\in \Sigma$.
If $y\in \partial \Sigma$, then $\Theta(\|V\|, y)=1/2$ and, by \eqref{eq: boundary monotonicity}, we can conclude \eqref{eq: density bound} holds for $0<r<r_K$.
Similarly if $y\in (\Sigma\setminus \partial\Sigma) \cap \partial M$.

Now fix $r\in(0, r_K)$. Since $y\in \Sigma\setminus \partial \Sigma$, then $\Theta(\|V\|, y)=1$ and $d\coloneqq \operatorname{dist}_{\mathrm{g}}(y, \partial M)>0$. If $d\geq r/4$, then $B_{r/8}(y)\Subset M\setminus \partial M$ and \eqref{eq: density bound} follows from \eqref{eq: interior monotonicity}. On the other hand, if $0<d< r/4$, then let $\hat y=\pi_{\partial M}(y)$ be the nearest boundary point to $y$. By interior monotonicity at $y$ at scale $d/2$, $\|V\|(B_{d/2}(y))\geq c_K d^n$. Since $B_{d/2}(y)\subset B_{2d}(\hat y)$, $\|V\|(B_{2d}(\hat y))\geq c_K(2d)^n$. By boundary monotonicity \eqref{eq: boundary monotonicity} from scale $2d$ to scale $r/2$,
\[
	\frac{\|V\|(B_{r/2}(\hat y))}{(r/2)^n}\geq c_K\frac{\|V\|(B_{2d}(\hat y))}{(2d)^n}\geq c_K
\]
after absorbing exponential factors in the constant.
Since $B_{r/2}(\hat y)\subset B_r(y)$, then \eqref{eq: density bound} follows.

Finally, for a singular point $y\in \mathcal{S}$, choose regular points $\Sigma\ni y_\ell\to y$, and notice that for all sufficiently large $\ell$, $B_{r/2}(y_\ell)\subset B_r(y)$, so \eqref{eq: density bound} at $y$ follows from the statement for regular points we have just proven.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- remark-26: body_md -->
A quantitative version of the density lower bound in \Cref{lemma: density bound} follows from \cite[Theorem 9.22]{MenneScharrer}.
<!-- end remark-26 -->
<!-- remark-27: body_md -->
\label{rmk: Allard metric convergence}
	It is easy to see that the conclusion of \Cref{prop: compactness stationary FB} still holds if each $\Sigma_k$ is a singular properly embedded free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $(M, \mathrm{g}_k)$ where the metrics $\mathrm{g}_k$ converge in $C^2_{\mathrm{loc}}$ to a smooth metric $\mathrm{g}$ on $M$.
<!-- end remark-27 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop: existence VarTan}
Let $\Sigma$ be a singular properly embedded free-boundary minimal hypersurface in $M$ and let $V=|\Sigma|$ be the associated multiplicity-one varifold. If $x\in \operatorname{spt}\|V\|=\overline{\Sigma}\subset M$, then $\operatorname{VarTan}(V, x)\neq \emptyset$. Moreover, 
\begin{enumerate}
	\item if $x\in M\setminus \partial M$ and $\mathbf{C}\in \operatorname{VarTan}(V, x)$, then $\mathbf{C}$ is a stationary integral cone in $(T_xM, \mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{Euc}})$ of density $\Theta(\|V\|, x)$;
	\item if $x\in \partial M$ and $\mathbf{C}\in \operatorname{VarTan}(V, x)$, then $\mathbf{C}$ is a free-boundary stationary integral cone in $(T^+_xM, \mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{Euc}})$ of density $\Theta(\|V\|, x)$, and $\overline{\mathbf{C}} = \mathbf{C}+(\theta_0)_\sharp\mathbf{C}$ is a stationary integral cone in $(T_xM, \mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{Euc}})$ which is invariant under $\theta_0$, where $\theta_0$ denotes the reflection across $T_x(\partial M)\subset T_xM$ in $(T_xM, \mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{Euc}})$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop: top stratum}
	Let $x\in\operatorname{spt}\|V\|$ and assume $\mathbf{C}\in \operatorname{VarTan}( V, x)$ is such that $\dim S(\widehat{\mathbf{C}})=n$. Then $x$ is a regular point of $V$ and $\operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)=\{\mathbf{C}\}$, i.e.  the tangent cone at $x$ is unique.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
For the sake of brevity, we shall focus on the case of $x\in \operatorname{spt}\|V\|\cap\partial M$. The interior case is almost identical to (and, indeed, slightly simpler than) (1) in the dichotomy below. So, for the rest of this proof, let  $x\in \operatorname{spt}\|V\|\cap\partial M$, and, for simplicity, let $H=T_xM$, $H_+=T^+_xM$, and $T=\partial H_+=T_x\partial M$. 

By the constancy theorem \cite{Simon1983}, $\overline{\mathbf{C}} = Q \vert \Pi \vert$ for some $n$-plane $\Pi$. By \Cref{prop: existence VarTan}, the doubled cone $\overline{\mathbf{C}}$ is invariant under the reflection $\theta_0$ across $T$. Hence, we know that the normal to $\Pi$ is an eigenvector of the reflection map.
Consequently, there are two options: 
\begin{enumerate}
   \item \textit{$\Pi = T_x\partial M$, in which case $Q$ is even and $\mathbf{C} = (Q/2) \vert T_x\partial M \vert$.} \\
Let the notation be as in \Cref{sec: tangent cones stratification}, so for $r>0$ the map $\Psi_{x, r}:H_+ \cap  B^{H}_{r^{-1}\rho_x}(0)\to M$ is the rescaling map in \eqref{eq: rescaling boundary} with inverse $\Phi_{x, r}$, and $\mathrm{g}_{x, r}=r^{-2}\Psi_{x, r}^*\mathrm{g}$ as a metric on (a subset of) $H_+$.
By definition (see \Cref{subsec: tangent cones} together with \Cref{rmk: extension to limits}), there exist positive numbers $r_i\to 0$ such that the free-boundary stationary integral varifolds $W_i\coloneqq (\Phi_{x, r_i})_\sharp V$ in $(H_+, \mathrm{g}_i)$ converge locally to $(Q/2) \vert T \vert\subset H_+$, where for simplicity we let $\mathrm{g}_i\coloneqq \mathrm{g}_{x, r_i}$. Let $d_i$ denote the $\mathrm{g}_i$-distance function to $T$ in $(H_+, \mathrm{g}_i)$. Moreover, for each $i$ and $R>0$, let $P^i_R$ be the intrinsic ball in $(T, \mathrm{g}_i|_{T})$ of radius $R$ centred at $0$ and $P_R$ the Euclidean ball in $T$. 
Since $\mathrm{g}_i\to \mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{Euc}}$ locally smoothly, given any $\rho_0>0$, there exists $i_0\geq 1$ such that 
\begin{equation}
\text{\parbox{0.85\textwidth}{\centering
		the normal exponential map of $T_x(\partial M)$ is a diffeomorphism of $P^i_{8\rho_0}\times[0, 8\rho_0)$ and $|\nabla^2_{\mathrm{g}_i}d_i|^2+ |\nabla^3_{\mathrm{g}_i}d_i|+|\operatorname{Rm}_{\mathrm{g}_i}|\leq 1$ for all $i\geq i_0$.
}}
\label{eq: uniform geometric bounds}
\end{equation}	
By \Cref{prop: compactness stationary FB} (and \Cref{rmk: extension to limits}), $\operatorname{spt} \|W_i\| \to T$ locally in Hausdorff distance. As $\mathrm{g}_i\to \mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{Euc}}$, for any compact set $K\subset H_+$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq: height to 0 parallel}
	\sup_{\operatorname{spt}\|W_i\|\cap K}d_i \to 0 \quad\text{as }i\rightarrow\infty\footnote{Here we are using that since $\mathrm{g}_i\to \mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{Euc}}$ locally smoothly, for any given compact set $K$, any given $\epsilon\in(0,1)$ and all sufficiently large $i$ the metrics satisfy $(1-\epsilon)\mathrm{g}_i\leq \mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{Euc}}\leq (1+\epsilon)\mathrm{g}_i$ on $K$. We shall use this fact repeatedly throughout the proof.}.
\end{equation}
Fix $\rho_0\in(0, 1]$ and the corresponding $i_0\geq 1$, and notice that by \eqref{eq: uniform geometric bounds}, the threshold radius $R_0$ and the threshold normalised tilt excess $\varepsilon_{0}$ in \Cref{thm: boundary sheeting} can be chosen to hold uniformly for all $i\geq i_0$. By varifold convergence to $(Q/2) \vert T \vert$, we can assume that for all $i \geq i_0$ we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq: mass bound parallel}
\|W_i\|(\mathcal{N}^+_{4\rho_0}(P^i_{4\rho_0}))\leq C(n, Q).
\end{equation}
For fixed $R\in(0, R_0/2)$, by \eqref{eq: mass bound parallel}, we can choose $\delta\in(0, \rho_0/2)$ such that the right hand side of \eqref{eq: tilt-height excess estimate} with $r=2R$ is strictly less than $\varepsilon_0^2/2$ for all $i\geq i_0$.
By \eqref{eq: height to 0 parallel} with $K=\overline{\mathcal{N}^+_{2\rho_0}}(P_{2R})$, by possibly increasing $i_0$, we can guarantee that for all $i \geq i_0$,
\begin{equation*}
	\operatorname{spt} \|W_i\|\cap \mathcal{N}^+_{2\rho_0}(P^i_{2R})\subset \mathcal{N}^+_{\delta/2}(P^i_{2R})                                    
\end{equation*}
Now fix the scale $r_*=r_{i_0}$ and write $\mathrm{g}_*\coloneqq \mathrm{g}_{x, r_{i_0}}$, $P^*_R\coloneqq P^{i_0}_R$, $W^*=W_{i_0}$, and $W^*_k\coloneqq (\Phi_{x, r_{i_0}})_\sharp V_k$. Notice that $W^*_k \to W^*$ locally as varifolds in the fixed Riemannian manifold-with-boundary $(H_+, \mathrm{g}_*)$. By \Cref{prop: compactness stationary FB} (and \Cref{rmk: extension to limits}), for all sufficiently large $k$
\begin{equation*}
	\operatorname{spt} \|W^*_k\|\cap \mathcal{N}^+_{2\rho_0}(P^*_{2R})\subset \mathcal{N}^+_{\delta}(P^*_{2R}),
\end{equation*}
and $W^*_k$ satisfies a mass bound analogous to \eqref{eq: mass bound parallel}.

Therefore, by \Cref{prop: boundary titl height estimate}, we obtain that, for all sufficiently large $k$, we have $$E_{W^*_k, P^*}(2R)^2\leq \varepsilon_0^2/2,$$ and \Cref{thm: boundary sheeting} applies. In particular, (ii) of \Cref{thm: boundary sheeting} applies and for all sufficiently large $k$ (by uniform equivalence of the metrics)
\begin{equation}\label{eq: preliminary sheeting parallel} 
	\operatorname{spt} \|W^*_k\|\cap \mathcal{N}^+_{2\rho_0}(P_{R/2})=\bigcup_{j=1}^{Q_k}F_{\partial M}(\operatorname{graph}(u_{k,j}))
\end{equation}
for some $Q_k\in \mathbb{N}$ and smooth functions $0\leq u_{k,1}<\dots<u_{k, Q_k}$, where $F_{\partial M}$ is the normal exponential map in the fixed rescaled metric. Moreover, $Q_k$ is bounded above uniformly by some $\hat Q$ because of the uniform mass bound.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 

Finally, the gradient estimate in \Cref{thm: boundary sheeting}(ii) implies that, after passing to a subsequence, for each $j=1, \dots, \hat Q$, the functions $u_{k,j}$ converge to some function $u_j$ in $C^{2,\alpha}$ and $0\leq u_1\leq \dots\leq u_{\hat{Q}}$ on $P_{R/4}$. Each $u_j$ satisfies the $\mathrm{g}_*$-minimal surface equation on $P_{R/4}$,  so if any two graphs touch they must coincide. After shrinking the neighbourhood further if needed, this shows that the support of $W^*$ in a neighbourhood of $0$ is given by a single smooth embedded minimal hypersurface, possibly with multiplicity, which must then be given by the density $Q/2$ at $0$.
After undoing all the rescalings, this shows that $x$ is a regular point of $V$, and that the tangent cone is unique. It also gives local smooth graphical convergence of the hypersurfaces $\Sigma_k$.

\medskip

\item \textit{$\Pi \subset T_xM$ is an $n$-plane invariant under reflection across $T_x\partial M$ and $\Pi \neq T_x \partial M$. \\}
Notice that then $\mathbf{C}=Q|\Pi^+|$. Let $L=\Pi\cap T$ and notice that $\Pi=L\oplus\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf{n}(x)\}$. For $\varrho\in(0, \operatorname{inj}_x(\partial M))$, let $\Gamma\coloneqq\exp^{\partial M}_x(L\cap B_{\varrho}(0))$, so that $T_x\Gamma = L$. Note that $\Gamma$ is a smooth $(n - 1)$-dimensional embedded disc in $\partial M$. By shrinking $\varrho$ if needed, the normal exponential map $F_{\partial M}$ is a diffeomorphism of $\Gamma\times [0, \varrho)$ into a smooth embedded two-sided $n$-dimensional disc $P$ in $M$, with $\partial_0P=\Gamma$. By construction, $P$ meets $\partial M$ orthogonally along $\Gamma$, and $T_y P = T_y \Gamma \oplus \mathrm{span}\{\mathbf{n}(y)\}$ for all $y\in \Gamma$. At $x$, we have $T_xP=\Pi$.
By \Cref{lemma: adapted neighbourhood} and \Cref{lemma: quantitative adapted neighbourhood}, there is $\varrho_1>0$ such that $P$ has an adapted $\varrho_1$-neighbourhood, and the corresponding $z_P, Z$ as in \Cref{def: adapted neighbourhood} satisfy the estimates in \Cref{lemma: quantitative adapted neighbourhood} for some $\Lambda>0$.

Now, as in (1), let $r_i\to 0$ be such that the free-boundary stationary integral varifolds $W_i\coloneqq (\Phi_{x, r_i})_\sharp V$ in $(H_+, \mathrm{g}_i)$ converge locally to $Q \vert \Pi^+ \vert\subset H_+$. Let $P^i\coloneqq \Phi_{x, r_i}(P)$ and notice that $P^i$ meets $T$ orthogonally in $(H_+, \mathrm{g}_i)$ along $\partial_0P^i$. 
Also, define $z_i\coloneqq r_i^{-1}z_P\circ \Psi_{x, r_i}$, and let $Z_i\coloneqq |\nabla^{\mathrm{g}_i}z_i|^{-1}_{\mathrm{g}_i}\nabla^{\mathrm{g}_i}z_i$. It is easy to check that the flow of $Z_i$ still defines an $r_i^{-1}\varrho_1$-adapted neighbourhood $U_i$ of $P^i$ in $(H_+, \mathrm{g}_i)$ and that $P^i\to \Pi^+, \quad z_i\to z_\Pi, \quad Z_i \to \nu_\Pi$
locally smoothly, where $\nu_\Pi$ is a (Euclidean) unit normal to $\Pi$, and $z_\Pi$ is the linear function vanishing on $\Pi$ with gradient $\nu_\Pi$. The corresponding flows also converge locally smoothly.
Finally, for $R>0$ and all $i$ sufficiently large, let $P^i_R$ be the intrinsic ball in $(P^i, \mathrm{g}_i|_{P^i})$ of radius $R$ centred at 0, which converges to the Euclidean ball $P_R$ in $\Pi^+$.
Again, since $\mathrm{g}_i\to \mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{Euc}}$ locally smoothly, there exists $i_1\geq 1$ such that \eqref{eq: geometric bounds orthogonal} holds with $\Lambda=1$ for all $i\geq i_1$. 

As in (1), by \Cref{prop: compactness stationary FB} (and \Cref{rmk: extension to limits}), $\operatorname{spt} \|W_i\| \to \Pi^+$ locally in Hausdorff distance. As $\mathrm{g}_i\to \mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{Euc}}$, for any compact set $K\subset H_+$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq: height to 0 orthogonal}
	\sup_{\operatorname{spt}\|W_i\|\cap K}|z_i| \to 0 \quad\text{as }i\rightarrow\infty\footnote{Again we are using uniform equivalence to $\mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{Euc}}$, in addition to the properties of $z_i$.}.
\end{equation}
Fix $\rho_1>0$ and the corresponding $i_1\geq 1$, and notice that the threshold distance $\delta_1$, the threshold radius $R_1$ and the threshold normalised tilt excess $\varepsilon_{1}$ in \Cref{thm: orthogonal sheeting} can be chosen to hold uniformly for all $i\geq i_1$. Similarly to (1), by varifold convergence we have uniform mass bounds on any fixed compact set, so for a given $R\in(0, R_1/2)$, we can choose $\delta\in(0, \delta_1/2)$ such that the right hand side of \eqref{eq: orthogonal tilt height estimate} with $r=2R$ is strictly less than $\varepsilon_1^2/2$ for all $i\geq i_1$.

Now the same argument as in (1) allows us to fix a scale $r_*=r_{i_1}$ for which we can apply the orthogonal sheeting theorem (\Cref{thm: orthogonal sheeting}(ii)) to rescalings of the varifolds $V_k=|\Sigma_k|$ and deduce that for all large $k$, the support of the rescalings $W^*_k$ in $\mathcal{N}^{Z^*}_{2\rho_1}(P_{R/2})$ is a union of $Q$ smooth $F_{Z^*}$-graphs over $P_{R/2}$. The gradient estimates in \Cref{thm: orthogonal sheeting}(ii) show that the graphs converge in $C^{2, \alpha}$ to graphs whose union induces $W^*$ on a smaller neighbourhood. A priori these graphs are weakly ordered but since they all satisfy a uniformly oblique boundary condition, the boundary and interior strong maximum principles for the minimal surface equation imply that  any two graphs which touch must coincide. Therefore, by shrinking the neighbourhood further if needed, the support of $W^*$ in a neighbourhood of $0$ is given by a single smooth embedded free-boundary minimal hypersurface, possibly with multiplicity. Again as in (1), this shows that $x$ is a regular point of $V$, and that the tangent cone is unique, together with local smooth graphical convergence of the $\Sigma_k$. 
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
Let $V$ be the multiplicity-one varifold induced by a stable singular properly embedded free-boundary minimal hypersurface $\Sigma$, or the varifold limit of a sequence of such objects. Let $x\in \operatorname{spt} \|V\|$ and let $\mathbf{C}\in \operatorname{VarTan}(V,x)$. Then $\operatorname{reg}(\mathbf{C})$ is a stable smooth (possibly free-boundary) minimal hypersurface in $(T_xM, \mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{Euc}})$.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Let the notation be as in \Cref{sec: tangent cones stratification} and the proof of \Cref{prop: top stratum}. For simplicity, let us assume $V=|\Sigma|$ for a stable singular properly embedded free-boundary minimal hypersurface $|\Sigma|$ and $x\in \overline\Sigma\cap \partial M$. By definition, there exists a sequence $r_i\to 0$ such that the multiplicity-one varifolds $|\Sigma_i|$ induced by stable singular free-boundary minimal hypersurfaces $\Sigma_i=\Phi_{x, r_i}(\Sigma)$ in the rescaled metrics $\mathrm{g}_i$ converge locally to $\mathbf{C}$. By tracking how the geometric quantities involved transform, we see that, for every $\varphi\in C_c^{0,1}(\Sigma_i)$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq: stability rescaled}
	\begin{aligned}
		\int_{\Sigma_i}|\nabla^{\mathrm{g_i}}_{\Sigma_i}\varphi|^2_{\mathrm{g}_i}- &  r_i^2\left((|A_\Sigma|^2_\mathrm{g}+\operatorname{Ric}_\mathrm{g}(\nu_\Sigma, \nu_\Sigma))\circ\Psi_{x, r_i}\right)\varphi^2 \, d\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{g}_i}^n\\
		&-r_i\int_{\partial \Sigma_i}(\mathrm{I\!I}^\mathrm{g}_{\partial M}(\nu_\Sigma, \nu_\Sigma)\circ\Psi_{x, r_i})\varphi^2\, d\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{g}_i}^{n-1}\geq 0.
	\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
By \Cref{cor: cap}, \eqref{eq: stability rescaled} holds for all compactly supported ambient Lipschitz test functions.

Now let $y\in \operatorname{reg}(\mathbf{C})$. Then any tangent cone $\mathbf D$ to $\mathbf{C}$ at $y$ is a constant multiplicity hyperplane or orthogonal half-plane. Therefore, after applying \Cref{prop: top stratum} to a diagonal sequence converging to $\mathbf{D}$, we see that the free-boundary minimal hypersurfaces in the diagonal sequence converge smoothly and graphically to $\mathbf{D}$ on a neighbourhood of $y$. In particular, we can pass \eqref{eq: stability rescaled} to the limit in a neighbourhood of $y$ and obtain that $\operatorname{reg}(\mathbf{C})$ satisfies the (Euclidean) stability inequality 
\begin{equation}
	\int_{\operatorname{reg}(\mathbf{C})}|A_{\mathbf{C}}|^2 \varphi^2\leq \int_{\operatorname{reg}(\mathbf{C})}|\nabla\varphi|^2
\end{equation}
for all compactly supported Lipschitz test functions $\varphi$.

Finally, the case where $V$ is a varifold limit of stable singular properly embedded free-boundary minimal hypersurfaces follows from the same proof after taking a further diagonal subsequence.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
Let $V$ be a varifold limit of a sequence $\{\Sigma_k\}_k$ of stable singular properly embedded free-boundary minimal hypersurfaces in $M$. Then the $(n-1)$-dimensional part of the singular stratum $\mathscr{S}_{n-1}\setminus\mathscr{S}_{n-2}$ of $V$ is empty.
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
As we stated above, we shall focus on the case where $x\in \operatorname{spt}\|V\|\cap \partial M$. As in the proof of \Cref{prop: top stratum}, we let $H=T_xM$, $H_+=T^+_xM$, $T=\partial H_+$, and 
$H_+^\circ=H_+\setminus T$.

Let $\mathbf{C}\in \operatorname{VarTan}(V, x)$ and assume that $\overline{\mathbf{C}}$ has an $(n - 1)$-dimensional spine, denoted by $L$. Since $\overline{\mathbf{C}}$ is invariant under reflection $\theta_0$ across $T = \partial H_+$, then $L$ is invariant under $\theta_0$. As the $(-1)$-eigenspace of the reflection is one-dimensional, exactly one of the following occurs: either $L \subset T$, or $\mathbf{n}_x \in L$, where $\mathbf{n}_x$ is the inward unit normal to $T$. 

If $\mathbf{n}_x \in L$, choose a nonzero point $z \in H_+^\circ\cap L$. In a sufficiently small ball centred at $z$, the reflected part of the cone lies outside $H_+$, and hence $\mathbf{C}$ agrees  with the full cone $\overline{\mathbf{C}}$ there. Since $\overline{\mathbf{C}}$ is invariant along $L$, its tangent cone at $z$ is again a classical cone with $(n - 1)$-dimensional spine. The usual interior slicing argument of \cite{SchoenSimon81} gives the contradiction. Hence, it remains to treat the genuinely free-boundary case $L \subset T$. 

\begin{comment}
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           
\end{comment}

Choose coordinates on $H\cong\mathbb{R}^{n+1}$ so that $H_+ = \{x_1 \geq 0\}$ and $T = \partial H_+ = \{x_1 = 0\}$. After rotation, assume that $L$ is given by $\{x_1 = x_{n + 1} = 0\} = \{0\} \times \mathbb{R}^{n - 1} \times \{0\} \subset T$. Write a point as $X = (x_1, y, x_{n + 1})$, where $y = (x_2, \ldots, x_{n}) \in \mathbb{R}^{n - 1}$. For $\alpha \in [0, \pi]$, define the $n$-dimensional half-hyperplane $$H^+_{\alpha} = \{(r \sin(\alpha), y, - r \cos(\alpha)) \; \colon \; r \geq 0, \, y \in \mathbb{R}^{n - 1} \}.$$ Then $\partial H^+_\alpha = L$ for every $\alpha$. Furthermore, $H^+_0, H^+_\pi \subset T$ (actually $T = H^+_0 \cup H^+_\pi$), while $H^+_{\pi/2} = \{x_{n + 1} = 0, x_1 \geq 0\}$ meets $T$ orthogonally. Set $\omega_\alpha = \sin(\alpha) e_1 - \cos(\alpha) e_{n + 1}$, so that $\omega_\alpha$ points from $L$ into $H_\alpha^+$. A choice of unit normal to $H^+_\alpha$ is given by $\nu_\alpha = \cos(\alpha)e_1 + \sin(\alpha) e_{n + 1}$, while the outward conormal of $H^+_\alpha$ along $L$ is $\eta_\alpha = - \omega_\alpha = - \sin(\alpha)e_1 + \cos(\alpha) e_{n + 1}$. Consequently, since the cone is invariant along $L$, it has the form
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbf{C} = q_0 \vert H^+_0 \vert + \sum_{i = 1}^m p_i \vert H^+_{\alpha_i} \vert + q_\pi \vert H^+_\pi \vert, 
\end{equation*}
with $0 < \alpha_1 < \alpha_2 < \ldots < \pi$, $p_i \in \mathbb{N}$, $q_0, q_\pi \in \mathbb{N} \cup \{0\}$. Because $\mathbf{C}$ is only free-boundary, we do not have the full vector balancing equation. However, testing the first variation near $L$ with a vector field equal to $e_{n + 1}$ which is tangent to $T$ gives the identity $q_0 - q_\pi + \sum_{i = 1}^m p_i \cos(\alpha_i) = 0$. There is no corresponding balancing condition for the $e_1$-component. 

We first prove that $\alpha_i = \pi/2$ for every $i$. Choose a diagonal sequence realising the tangent cone $\mathbf{C}$. Thus, after relabelling, $\Sigma_j = \Phi_{x, r_j}(\Sigma_{k_j})$ and $V_j = (\Phi_{x, r_j})_\sharp V_{k_j}$; then $V_j\to \mathbf{C}$ locally as varifolds. We also have the corresponding sequence of Riemannian metrics as in \Cref{prop: top stratum}. Every point of $\operatorname{spt}\|\mathbf{C}\| \setminus L$ has a planar tangent cone. On the one hand, at points of $H^+_{\alpha_i} \setminus L$, with $0 < \alpha_i < \pi$, we are in the interior of $H$, and hence interior sheeting \cite{SchoenSimon81} applies. On the other hand, at points of $(H^+_0 \cup H_\pi^+) \setminus L$, boundary sheeting (cf. \Cref{thm: boundary sheeting}) applies. Consequently, $\Sigma_j \rightarrow \mathbf{C}$ smoothly and graphically on compact subsets of $\overline{H}_+\setminus L$. In other words, on every fixed annulus around the spine $L$, the graphical sheets, their tangent planes, and their unoriented normals converge (locally) uniformly to those of $\mathbf{C}$. 

For $\tau > 0$, define the following $\tau$-tubular neighbourhood $\mathcal{L}_\tau$ of the spine by 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{L}_\tau = \{(x_1, y, x_{n + 1}) \in B_{3/2}^+ \; \colon \; x_1^2 + x_{n + 1}^2 < \tau^2, \vert y \vert < 1\}.
\end{equation*}
Choose $\zeta\in C_c^1(B_2)$ such that $  0\leq\zeta\leq1,$ $ \zeta\equiv1 $ on $B_{3/2},$  and $|D\zeta|\leq 3.$ Since the metrics are converging locally in $C^2$, applying the stability inequality \eqref{eq: stability rescaled} for $\Sigma_j$, reabsorbing the boundary terms using \Cref{lemma: trace inequality}, and then using uniform equivalence of $\mathrm{g}_j$ to $\mathrm{g}_{\mathrm{Euc}}$ gives a uniform bound $\int_{\Sigma_j \cap B_{3/2}^+} \vert A_j \vert^2 \leq C$.\\ Furthermore, cover $L\cap\{|y|<1\}$ by at most $C\tau^{1-n}$ balls of radius $2\tau$ with centres on $L\subset T$. Boundary-centred monotonicity and the uniform mass bound give mass at most $C\tau^n$ in each corresponding ball of radius $4\tau$. Hence $\mathcal H^n_{\mathrm g_j}(\Sigma_j\cap\mathcal L_\tau)\leq C\tau$, uniformly for all sufficiently large $j$ and $0<\tau<\tau_0$. 
Therefore, we obtain
\begin{align}  \label{eq: L1 curvature in spine tube}
    \int_{\Sigma_j\cap\mathcal L_\tau}
    |A_j| \leq \left(\int_{\Sigma_j\cap B_{3/2}^{+}} |A_j|^2 \right)^{1/2} \left( \mathcal H_{\mathrm g_j}^{n} \bigl(\Sigma_j\cap\mathcal L_\tau\bigr)\right)^{1/2} \leq C\tau^{1/2}.
\end{align}
Let now $\pi:H_+\rightarrow L$ be given by $\pi(x_1,y,x_{n+1})=y.$ For $y\in\mathbb R^{n-1}$, define $ P^+_y \coloneqq \pi^{-1}(y) = \left\{(x_1,y,x_{n+1}) \, \colon x_1\geq0 \right\}$ and $D_{\tau, y}^+ = \{(x_1, y, x_{n + 1}) \in P_y^+ \, \colon x_1^2 + x_{n + 1}^2 \leq \tau^2\}$. We denote the curved boundary of this half-disk by $\Lambda_{\tau, y} = \{(x_1, y, x_{n + 1}) \, \colon x_1\geq 0,\ x_1^2 + x_{n + 1}^2 = \tau^2\}$. Since $\mathcal S_j$ has locally finite $\mathcal{H}^{n-2}$ measure, we have $\mathcal H^{n-1}(\mathcal S_j)=0,$ and hence, since $\pi$ is Lipschitz, $\mathcal H^{n-1}\bigl(\pi(\mathcal S_j)\bigr)=0.$ Consequently, for almost every $y \in B_{1/2}^{n-1}$, the point $y$ does not belong to $\pi(\mathcal{S}_j)$ and, by Sard's theorem, is a regular value of both $\pi|_{\Sigma_j\setminus\mathcal S_j}$ and $\pi|_{\partial\Sigma_j}.$ Additionally, the intersection with $\Lambda_{\tau, y}$ is transverse for all sufficiently large $j$. For all such $y$, the slice $\Gamma_{j,y} \coloneqq \Sigma_j\cap D_{\tau, y}^+$ is a one-dimensional, smooth manifold with $\partial \Gamma_{j, y} \subset (\Sigma_j \cap \Lambda_{\tau, y}) \cup (\partial \Sigma_j \cap D_{\tau, y}^+)$. Notice that a point of $(\Sigma_j \setminus \partial \Sigma_j) \cap T$ is an interior point of $\Gamma_{j, y}$ rather than an endpoint. In particular, every connected component of $\Gamma_{j, y}$ is either a smooth embedded circle, or a smooth embedded arc with exactly two endpoints. In the latter case, the unordered pair of endpoint types is one of $\{\Lambda_{\tau, y}, \Lambda_{\tau, y}\}$, $\{\Lambda_{\tau, y}, \partial \Sigma_j\}$, or $\{\partial \Sigma_j, \partial \Sigma_j\}$. No other case arises: the slice does not meet $\mathcal{S}_j$ or the curved part of $\partial B_{3/2}^+$, while properness prevents connected components from terminating or accumulating in the interior of $D_{\tau, y}^+$. 

Let $N_j$ denote a continuous choice of Euclidean unit normal to $\Sigma_j$, and regard $[N_j] \colon \Sigma_j \rightarrow \mathbb{RP}^n$ as the unoriented Gauss map. For a curve $\gamma \subset \Sigma_j$, write 
\[
\mathrm{osc}_\gamma[N_j] = \sup_{a, b \in \gamma} d_{\mathbb{RP}^n}([N_j(a)], [N_j(b)]). 
\]
Suppose for the sake of contradiction that $\alpha_{i_0} \neq \pi/2$ for some $i_0$. For every regular slice, choose one of the outer endpoints $a_{j, y} \in \Sigma_j \cap \Lambda_{\tau, y}$ corresponding to the ray $H^+_{\alpha_{i_0}}$, and let $\gamma_{j, y} \subset \Gamma_{j, y}$ be the connected component containing it. Since $\gamma_{j, y}$ has an endpoint, it is an arc. Denote its other endpoint by $b_{j, y}$. Let $\mathcal{A}_{\mathbf{C}}  \coloneqq \{\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_m\} \cup \{0:q_0>0\} \cup \{\pi:q_\pi>0\}$ and define
\begin{align} \label{eq: normal separation constant}
\delta_{\mathbf C}
    \coloneqq
    \min\Bigl\{d_{\mathbb{RP}^n} \bigl([\nu_{\alpha_{i_0}}], \mathbb P(e_1^\perp)\bigr), d_{\mathbb{RP}^n}\bigl([\nu_{\alpha_{i_0}}],[\nu_\alpha]\bigr): \alpha\in\mathcal A_{\mathbf C},\ \alpha\neq\alpha_{i_0}\Bigr\}, 
\end{align}
where the latter family is omitted if empty. Since $d_{\mathbb{RP}^n}([\nu_{\alpha_{i_0}}], \mathbb P(e_1^\perp)) = \vert \alpha_{i_0} - \pi/2 \vert > 0$, and we have finitely many rays, $\delta_{\mathbf{C}} > 0$. We now have three cases for $b_{j, y}$: 
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
    \item If $b_{j,y}\in\partial\Sigma_j$, then the free-boundary
    condition gives $d_{\mathbb{RP}^n}\bigl([N_j(b_{j,y})],\mathbb P(e_1^\perp)\bigr)=o_j(1),$ while smooth convergence at the outer endpoint gives $[N_j(a_{j,y})] \rightarrow [\nu_{\alpha_{i_0}}].$
    Hence, for all sufficiently large $j$, $\operatorname{osc}_{\gamma_{j,y}}[N_j] \geq \delta_{\mathbf C}/2$. 
    \item If $b_{j,y}\in\Lambda_{\tau,y}$ corresponds to a ray
    $H_\alpha^+$ with $\alpha\neq\alpha_{i_0}$, then $[N_j(a_{j,y})]\rightarrow [\nu_{\alpha_{i_0}}],$ and $[N_j(b_{j,y})]\rightarrow[\nu_\alpha].$ Therefore, for all sufficiently large $j$, $\operatorname{osc}_{\gamma_{j,y}}[N_j]\geq \delta_{\mathbf C}/2.$
    \item Suppose finally that $b_{j,y}\in\Lambda_{\tau,y}$ corresponds to the same ray $H_{\alpha_{i_0}}^+$. Set $E=\operatorname{span}\{e_1,e_{n+1}\}$, and let $N_j$ be as above. Since $y$ is a regular value of $\pi|_{\Sigma_j}$, we have $T_p\gamma_{j,y} = \ker D(\pi|_{\Sigma_j})_p = T_p\Sigma_j\cap E,$ and $\dim T_p\gamma_{j,y}=1.$ In particular, $\operatorname{proj}_E N_j\neq0$ along $\gamma_{j,y}$: otherwise $N_j\in E^\perp$, and hence $E\subset N_j^\perp=T_p\Sigma_j$, contradicting the fact that the kernel above is one-dimensional. Thus, $\widehat N_{j,y} \coloneqq |\operatorname{proj}_E N_j|^{-1} \operatorname{proj}_E N_j$ is a unit normal to the planar curve $\gamma_{j,y}$. Orient this curve from $a_{j,y}$ to $b_{j,y}$, and let $T_{j,y}$ denote its Euclidean unit tangent. Smooth convergence at the two outer endpoints gives, for all sufficiently large $j$, $\langle T_{j,y}(a_{j,y}), \omega_{\alpha_{i_0}}\rangle <- 1/2, $ and $\langle T_{j,y}(b_{j,y}),\omega_{\alpha_{i_0}}\rangle > 1/2,$ since the curve enters $D_{\tau,y}^+$ at the first endpoint and leaves it at the second. If $\operatorname{osc}_{\gamma_{j,y}}[N_j]$ were sufficiently small, then $[\widehat N_{j,y}]$ would remain close to $[\nu_{\alpha_{i_0}}]$, and hence the tangent line would remain close to $[\omega_{\alpha_{i_0}}]$. In particular, for some fixed $c>0$, $\big|\langle T_{j,y},\omega_{\alpha_{i_0}}\rangle\big| \geq c$ along $\gamma_{j,y}.$ This contradicts the intermediate value theorem, since the scalar product is negative at $a_{j,y}$ and positive at $b_{j,y}$. Therefore, $\operatorname{osc}_{\gamma_{j,y}}[N_j] \geq\delta_{\mathrm{turn}}$, for some $\delta_{\mathrm{turn}}>0$ independent of $j,y,\tau$.
\end{enumerate}
Taking $ \beta \coloneqq \min \{\delta_{\mathbf C}/2,\delta_{\mathrm{turn}}\}>0,$ we conclude that $\operatorname{osc}_{\gamma_{j,y}}[N_j] \geq\beta$ for all sufficiently large $j$ and almost every regular $y\in B_{1/2}^{n-1}$.

Next, there exists a sequence $\varepsilon_j \rightarrow 0$ such that, along every $\mathrm g_j$-unit speed curve in $\Sigma_j$, there holds $\vert \partial_s N_j \vert \leq C(\vert A_j \vert + \varepsilon_j)$. Thus, we obtain 
\[
\beta \leq C \int_{\gamma_{j, y}} (\vert A_j \vert + \varepsilon_j) \, d\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{g}_j}^1, \qquad \text{and} \qquad
\beta \leq C \int_{\Sigma_j \cap P^+_y \cap \mathcal{L}_\tau} (\vert A_j \vert + \varepsilon_j) \, d\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{g}_j}^1, 
\]
for almost every $y \in B_{1/2}^{n  - 1}$. Integrating and using coarea, we obtain
\begin{align*}
    \beta \mathcal L^{n-1} \bigl(B_{1/2}^{n-1}\bigr)
    &\leq C\int_{B_{1/2}^{n-1}} \int_{\Sigma_j\cap P^+_y\cap\mathcal L_\tau} \bigl(|A_j|+\varepsilon_j\bigr)\, d\mathcal H_{\mathrm{g}_j}^{1}\,dy \leq C\int_{\Sigma_j\cap\mathcal L_\tau} |A_j|\,d\mathcal H_{\mathrm g_j}^{n} + C\varepsilon_j \mathcal H_{\mathrm{g}_j}^{n} \bigl(\Sigma_j\cap\mathcal L_\tau\bigr),
\end{align*}
where we have used the uniform bound $ J_{n-1} (\pi|_{\Sigma_j})\leq C$. We conclude that $\beta \mathcal L^{n-1}(B_{1/2}^{n-1}) \leq C\tau^{1/2} + C\varepsilon_j\tau.$ First let $j\to\infty$, with $\tau>0$ fixed. We obtain $\beta \mathcal L^{n-1} (B_{1/2}^{n-1}) \leq C\tau^{1/2}.$ Letting $\tau\downarrow0$, the right-hand side tends to zero, whereas the left-hand side is strictly positive. This contradiction proves that $\alpha_i= \pi/2$ for every $i$. 

We have proven that $ \mathbf{C} = q_0|H^+_0| + p|H^+_{\pi/2}| + q_\pi|H^+_\pi|,$ where $p=\sum_i p_i.$ Appealing to the free-boundary balancing equation, we infer $ q_0-q_\pi=0.$ Consequently, $q_0=q_\pi=q$
and $q|H^+_0|+q|H^+_\pi|=q|T|.$ Thus, $\mathbf{C} = p \vert H_{\pi/2}^+ \vert + q \vert T \vert$. 

                                                                                                                                                                                              
A completely analogous argument allows us to treat the mixed case $p, q \geq 0$. More precisely, one can show that $p q = 0$, in which case either $\mathbf{C} = q \vert T \vert$, or $\mathbf{C} = p \vert H_{\pi/2}^+ \vert$. Doubling and appealing to \Cref{thm: boundary sheeting}, or \Cref{thm: orthogonal sheeting}, we conclude.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lemma: cutoff Hn-1}
	Let $\Gamma$ be a relatively closed subset of $D\cup E$ and assume $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\Gamma)=0$. For every compact set $K\subset D\cup E$ there exist $\chi_j\in C^\infty(D\cup E)$ with $0\leq \chi_j\leq 1$, each $\chi_j$ vanishing on a relative neighbourhood of $\Gamma\cap K$ in $D\cup E$ such that $\chi_j\to 1$ locally on $(D\cup E)\cap K\setminus \Gamma$ and $\int_{D\cap K} |\nabla\chi_j|\to 0$.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
This follows from \cite[Theorem 4.15(v)]{Evans2015}.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lemma: removabillity}
Let $\Gamma$ be a relatively closed subset of $D\cup E$ and assume $\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\Gamma)=0$. Let $u$ be a Lipschitz function on $D\cup E$ such that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq: strict bounds}
	\|u\|_{L^\infty(D\cup E)}\leq c_0<C_0, \quad \operatorname{Lip}(u; D\cup E)\leq c_1<C_1,
\end{equation}
and suppose that the identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq: EL equation}
	\int_D\bigl(D_q\mathcal{L}(y, u, \nabla u)\cdot \nabla\varphi+\partial_z\mathcal{L}(y, u, \nabla u)\varphi\bigr)\, dy=0
\end{equation}	
holds for every test function $\varphi\in C^1(D\cup E)$ such that $\operatorname{spt} \varphi \Subset (D\cup E)\setminus \Gamma$.\\
Then \eqref{eq: EL equation} holds for every $\varphi\in C^1(D\cup E)$ such that $\operatorname{spt} \varphi \Subset (D\cup E)$ and $u\in C^\infty_{\mathrm{loc}}(D\cup E)$. In particular, $u$ is a classical solution of
\begin{equation}\label{eq: EL nonvar}
-\partial_{y_i}(\partial_{q_i}\mathcal L(y, u, \nabla u))+\partial_z\mathcal L(y, u, \nabla u)=0
\end{equation}
and 
\begin{equation}\label{eq: EL BC}
D_q\mathcal{L}(y, u, \nabla u)\cdot \eta_D=0\quad \text{on $E$}
\end{equation}
where $\eta_D$ is the outward unit normal to $D$ along $E$.
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Fix $\varphi\in C^1(D\cup E)$ such that $\operatorname{spt} \varphi \Subset (D\cup E)$ and let $K=\operatorname{spt} \varphi$. Then let $\chi_j$ be the cutoff functions provided by \Cref{lemma: cutoff Hn-1}. Then $\chi_j\varphi$ is an admissible test function for \eqref{eq: EL equation}, so that
\begin{equation}\label{eq: EL variational}
0 = \int_D\chi_j \bigl(D_q\mathcal{L}(y, u, \nabla u)\cdot \nabla\varphi+\partial_z\mathcal{L}(y, u, \nabla u)\varphi\bigr)\, dy+\int_D\varphi D_q\mathcal L(y, u, \nabla u)\cdot \nabla \chi_j\, dy.
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq: strict bounds} and smoothness of $\mathcal L$, there is some large constant $C<+\infty$ such that \[\|D_q\mathcal{L}(y, u, \nabla u)\|_{L^\infty(D\cap K)}+\|\partial_z\mathcal{L}(y, u, \nabla u)\|_{L^\infty(D\cap K)}\leq C<+\infty.\]
Hence, 
\[
\left|\int_D\varphi D_q\mathcal L(y, u, \nabla u)\cdot \nabla \chi_j\, dy\right|\leq C\|\varphi\|_{L^\infty}\int_{D\cap K}|\nabla \chi_j| \to 0
\]
by \Cref{lemma: cutoff Hn-1}. The first integral in \eqref{eq: EL variational} converges by the dominated convergence theorem. Therefore \eqref{eq: EL equation} holds for $\varphi$, and, hence, by arbitrariness of $\varphi$ for all compactly supported $C^1$ functions on $D\cup E$.

Smoothness of $u$ in the interior then follows from elliptic regularity theory since the divergence form operator associated to \eqref{eq: EL equation} is uniformly elliptic by \eqref{eq: ellipticity}, the coefficients of $\mathcal{L}$ are assumed to be smooth and $u$ is Lipschitz. Along $E$, the natural boundary operator is $\mathcal{B}(y, z, q)\coloneqq D_q\mathcal L(y,z, q)\cdot \eta_D(y)$. Notice that it is uniformly oblique as
\[
D_q\mathcal B(y, z, q)\cdot \eta_D=\partial_{q_iq_j}\mathcal L(y, z, q)\eta_D^i\eta_D^j\geq \lambda_1|\eta_D|^2=\lambda_1>0.
\]
By \cite[Theorem 1]{Lieberman88}, there exists $\alpha\in(0,1)$ such that $u\in C^{1, \alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(D\cup E)$. By standard elliptic theory, smoothness follows from $C^{2, \alpha}$ regularity, which is a consequence of the boundary Schauder estimates of \cite{LiebTru86}.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- remark-28: body_md -->
Notice that \Cref{lemma: removabillity} is considerably weaker and more elementary than Simon’s removable singularity theorem in \cite{SimonRemovable77}. Indeed,  in \Cref{lemma: removabillity} the function $u$ is already defined and Lipschitz across the deleted set $\Gamma$, so only the weak Euler–Lagrange identity must be extended. Simon on the other hand assumes no a priori boundedness or continuity of the solution near $\Gamma$ and proves the existence of a classical extension. However, Simon's theorem as formulated in \cite{SimonRemovable77} does not cover integrands having $z$-dependence or oblique boundary conditions.
<!-- end remark-28 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{L: Riesz potential}
	Let $n\geq 2$, and let $E\subset\mathbb R^{n+1}$ be compact with $  \mathcal H^{n-2}(E)<\infty.$ Then there exists an upper semicontinuous function $\psi:\mathbb R^{n+1} \rightarrow[-\infty,\infty)$ such that
	\[
	\{\psi=-\infty\}=E,\qquad
	\psi\in C^\infty(\mathbb R^{n+1}\setminus E),
	\]
	and
	\[
	\mathrm{tr}_{P}D^2\psi(x)\geq0
	\qquad
	\text{for every }x\in\mathbb R^{n+1}\setminus E
	\text{ and }P\in G(n,n+1).
	\]
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Let $K$ be the fundamental solution of the Laplacian in $\mathbb{R}^n$, so that $K(r)\sim r^{2-n}$ for $n\geq 3$ and $K(r)\sim -\log r$ for $n=2$.
	We first consider $n\geq3$. Define $U_{n - 2}^\sigma(x)\coloneqq\int K(|x-y|)\,d\sigma(y)$ for a finite positive measure $\sigma$. We shall use the following elementary fact (see \cite[Theorem 4.13(a)]{Falconer}).
	
	\begin{fact}\label{fact: falconer Riesz energy}
		Let $F\subset\mathbb R^{n+1}$ be Borel, and let $\sigma$ be a
		nonzero finite positive measure supported on $F$. If 
		\[
		I_{n - 2}(\sigma) \coloneqq \iint K(|x-y|)\,d\sigma(x)d\sigma(y)<\infty,
		\]
		then $\mathcal H^{n - 2}(F)=\infty.$                                                                             
	\end{fact}
	                                  
	                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    
	   
	                                                                                                        
	   
	                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               
	   
	              
	                                                           
	                                                                                
	            
	   
	              
	   
	                                                                                                                   
	   
	                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  
	            
	
	Let now $\lambda\coloneqq\mathcal H^{n - 2} \mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} E,$ which is a finite positive measure by hypothesis. Set $ Z\coloneqq\{x\in E:U_{n - 2}^\lambda(x)<\infty\}, $ and $Z_m\coloneqq\{x\in E:U_{n - 2}^\lambda(x)\leq m\},$ so that $Z_m \subset Z$ and $Z = \bigcup_m Z_m$. We claim that $ \mathcal H^{n - 2}(Z)=0.$\\ Indeed, if $\lambda(Z_m)>0$ for some $m$, then the nonzero measure $ \sigma\coloneqq\lambda\mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} Z_m$ satisfies
	\[
	I_{n - 2}(\sigma) =\int_{Z_m}U_{n - 2}^\sigma\,d\lambda \leq\int_{Z_m}U_{n - 2}^\lambda\,d\lambda \leq m\lambda(Z_m)<\infty,
	\]
	and \Cref{fact: falconer Riesz energy} would then imply $\mathcal H^{n - 2}(Z_m)=\infty$, contradicting $Z_m\subset E$ and $\mathcal H^{n - 2}(E)<\infty$. Hence $\lambda(Z_m)=0$ for every $m$, and therefore $\mathcal H^{n - 2}(Z)=\lambda(Z)=0.$
	
	Thus $U_{n - 2}^\lambda(\cdot)=+\infty$ at every point of $E\setminus Z$. To conclude the argument, we shall now construct another finite positive measure $\nu$ such that $U_{n - 2}^\nu(x)=+\infty$ for all $x\in Z$ as well. In the end, we shall define $\mu\coloneqq \lambda+\nu$ and take $\psi = -U^\mu_{n-2}$, which, as we will show, has the desired properties.
	
	Since $\mathcal H^{n - 2}(Z)=0$, for each $k\geq1$ we may choose a countable cover
	\[
	Z\subset\bigcup_i B_{r_{k,i}}(a_{k,i}),
	\qquad a_{k,i}\in Z,
	\]
	such that $ \sum_i r_{k,i}^{n - 2}<2^{-k}.$ Define $\nu_k\coloneqq\sum_i r_{k,i}^{n - 2}\,\delta_{a_{k,i}}$, and $\nu\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^\infty\nu_k$.
	Then $\nu$ is a finite positive measure supported on $E$, since $\nu(\mathbb R^{n+1}) \leq\sum_{k=1}^\infty2^{-k}<\infty$.
	For every $x\in Z$ and every $k$, there is an $i$ such that $|x-a_{k,i}|<r_{k,i}$, from which we deduce $ U_{n - 2}^{\nu_k}(x) \geq |x-a_{k,i}|^{2 -n} r_{k,i}^{n - 2} \geq 1.$ It follows that
	\[
	U_{n - 2}^\nu(x) = \sum_{k=1}^\infty U_{n - 2}^{\nu_k}(x) =+\infty \qquad\text{for every }x\in Z.
	\]
	
	Consequently, the finite positive measure $\mu\coloneqq\lambda+\nu$ is supported on $E$ and satisfies $U_{n - 2}^\mu(x)=+\infty$ for every $x \in E$. Define now $\psi(x) \coloneqq -U_{n - 2}^\mu(x)$ so that $\psi=-\infty$ on $E$. If $x \notin E$, then $\operatorname{dist}(x,E)>0,$ and hence $U_{n - 2}^\mu(x)<\infty$. Thus $\{\psi=-\infty\}=E.$ Moreover, on every compact subset of $\mathbb R^{n+1}\setminus E$, the kernel and all of its derivatives are uniformly bounded for $y\in E$; differentiation under the integral therefore gives $\psi\in C^\infty(\mathbb R^{n+1}\setminus E).$
	
	It remains to verify the Hessian inequality. For $z\neq0$, writing
	$e=z/|z|$, one computes
	\[
	D^2\bigl(-|z|^{2-n}\bigr) = (n-2)|z|^{-n}\bigl(I-n\,e\otimes e\bigr).
	\]
	Hence, for every $P\in G(n,n+1)$, we have $\mathrm{tr}_P D^2\bigl(-|z|^{2-n}\bigr) = (n-2)|z|^{-n} \bigl(n-n|\pi_Pe|^2\bigr) = n(n-2)|z|^{-n}\bigl(1-|\pi_Pe|^2\bigr)\geq0$.
	It follows that, for $x\notin E$,
	\[
	\begin{split}
		\mathrm{tr}_P D^2\psi(x)
		&=
		\frac{n}{\omega_n}
		\int_E
		\frac{1-|\pi_Pe_{x-y}|^2}{|x-y|^n}\,d\mu(y)
		\geq0,
	\end{split}
	\]
	where $e_{x-y}\coloneqq(x-y)/|x-y|$.
	
	                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        
	
	If $n=2$, then $\mathcal H^0(E)<\infty$, so that $E=\{a_1,\ldots,a_N\}$ is finite. In this case take
	\[
	\mu\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^N\delta_{a_j}, \quad \text{and} \quad \psi(x)\coloneqq\int K(|x-y|)\,d\mu(y) =\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{j=1}^N\log|x-a_j|.
	\]
	Clearly $\{\psi=-\infty\}=E$ and $\psi\in C^\infty(\mathbb R^3\setminus E)$. Since $D^2\log|z| = |z|^{-2}\bigl(I-2e\otimes e\bigr),$ for every $P\in G(2,3)$, $ \mathrm{tr}_P D^2\log|z| = 2|z|^{-2}\bigl(1-|\pi_Pe|^2\bigr)\geq0.$
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- fact-1: body_md -->
\label{fact: falconer Riesz energy}
		Let $F\subset\mathbb R^{n+1}$ be Borel, and let $\sigma$ be a
		nonzero finite positive measure supported on $F$. If 
		\[
		I_{n - 2}(\sigma) \coloneqq \iint K(|x-y|)\,d\sigma(x)d\sigma(y)<\infty,
		\]
		then $\mathcal H^{n - 2}(F)=\infty.$
<!-- end fact-1 -->
<!-- remark-29: body_md -->
The function $\psi$, extended by $-\infty$ on $E$, is \emph{$n$-plurisubharmonic} in the sense of \cite[\S 9]{HarveyLawson2014}. Consequently the set $E$ is \emph{$C^\infty$ $n$-pluripolar} (again, see \cite[\S 9]{HarveyLawson2014}).
<!-- end remark-29 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lemma: Riesz Riemannian}
	Let $n\geq 2$.
	\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
		\item Let $\mathrm{g}$ be a smooth Riemannian metric on a neighbourhood of $\overline{B}_2$. There exists $r_0\in(0,1)$ such that for every compact set $E\subset \overline{B}_{r_0}$ with $\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(E)<\infty$ there is a function $v\in C^\infty(B_{2r_0}\setminus E)$, $v\geq 1$ such that $v(x)\to +\infty$ as $x\to E$ and $\mathrm{tr}_P\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}v(x)\leq 0$ for all $x\in B_{2r_0}\setminus E$ and every $P\in G(n, n+1)$.
		
		\item Let $\mathrm{g}$ be a smooth Riemannian metric on a neighbourhood of $\overline{C}_2$ of the form $\mathrm{g}=dt^2+h_{ij}(y, t)dy^idy^j$, where $(h_{ij})$ is smooth and positive-definite for all $t\in[0, 2]$. There exists $r_0\in(0,1)$ such that for every compact set $E\subset \overline{B}^n_{r_0}\times\{0\}$ with $\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(E)<\infty$ there is a function $v\in C^\infty(C_{2r_0}\setminus E)$, $v\geq 1$ such that $v(x)\to +\infty$ as $x\to E$ and $\mathrm{tr}_P\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}v(x)\leq 0$ for all $x\in C_{2r_0}\setminus E$ and every $P\in G(n, n+1)$, and $\partial_tv=0$ on $\partial_0C_{2r_0}\setminus E$.
	\end{enumerate}
    In both cases, the extension of $v$ by $+\infty$ on $E$ is lower semicontinuous on $B_{2r_0}$ and $C_{2r_0}$, respectively.
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
In order to deal with the errors arising from the Riemannian metric $\mathrm{g}$, it is convenient to introduce a strictly superharmonic (with respect to the Euclidean metric) perturbation of the Riesz kernel in \Cref{L: Riesz potential}.
	For $\lambda>0$, choose $r_*>0$ such that $\lambda r_*<n$, and define
	\begin{equation}\label{eq: perturbation Riesz kernel}
		K_{\lambda}(r)\coloneqq \int_r^{r_*}s^{1-n}e^{\lambda s}\, ds\quad \text{for } 0<r<r_*, 
	\end{equation}
	and notice that, as $r\downarrow 0$, $K_{\lambda}$ has the same leading order behaviour as the Riesz kernel in \Cref{L: Riesz potential}.
	
	Now, for (a), by shrinking $r_0$, let us assume that $B_{3r_0}$ is strongly $\mathrm{g}$-convex and for $a\in B_{r_0}$ the distance $r_a(\cdot)=\operatorname{dist}_\mathrm{g}(\cdot, a)$ is smooth on $B_{2r_0}\setminus \{a\}$ and satisfies $|\nabla_\mathrm{g}^2r_a-r_a^{-1}(\mathrm{g}-dr_a\otimes dr_a)|\leq Cr_a$ (which is a consequence of the Hessian comparison theorem in \cite[Theorem 11.7]{LeeRiem}).
	Then, if $P\in G(n, n+1)$ and $\alpha=|\pi^\mathrm{g}_P\nabla^\mathrm{g}r_a|_\mathrm{g}^2\in[0,1]$, we have
	\begin{equation}\label{eq: interior trace}
		\begin{aligned}
			\mathrm{tr}_P\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}K_{\lambda}(r_a) \leq K''_{\lambda}(r_a)+\frac{n-1}{r_a}K'_{\lambda}(r_a)+Cr_a|K'_{\lambda}(r_a)| \leq (-\lambda+Cr_a)|K'_{\lambda}(r_a)|.
		\end{aligned}
	\end{equation}
	Take $\lambda=1$ and reduce $r_0$ so that $Cr_a<1$; then $\mathrm{tr}_P\nabla^2_\mathrm{g}K_{1}(r_a)\leq 0$ for all $P\in G(n, n+1)$.
	
	If we let $\mu$ be the finite positive measure given in the proof of \Cref{L: Riesz potential}, and 
	\[
	v(x)\coloneqq 1+\int_E K_{1}(\operatorname{dist}_\mathrm{g}(x,y))\, d\mu(y),
	\]
	then it is easy to check that, after shrinking $r_0$ if needed, $v$ has all the required properties, since $\operatorname{dist}_{\mathrm{g}}$ is uniformly equivalent to the Euclidean distance on $B_{3r_0}$ with constants going to 1 as $r_0\to 0$.
    In particular, the integral diverges on $E$. Its extension by $+\infty$ on $E$ is lower semicontinuous by Fatou's lemma, which gives $v(x)\to+\infty$ as $x\to E$.
    
	For (b), write $h_t\coloneqq h_{ij}(y, t)dy^idy^j$ on $C_2$ and let $\hat{\mathrm{g}}\coloneqq dt^2+h_0(y)$ on the doubled cylinder $\hat{C}_2\coloneqq B^n_2(0)\times (-2,2)$. For $a=(a', 0)\in E$, let $\hat{r}_a(y, t)\coloneqq \operatorname{dist}_{\hat{\mathrm{g}}}((y, t), (a',0))=(\operatorname{dist}_{h_0}(y, a')^2+t^2)^{1/2}$. Then notice that $\partial_t\hat r_a(y, 0)=0$ for $y\neq a'$.
	
	The interior computation in \eqref{eq: interior trace} implies that for every $P\in G(n, n+1)$, we have $\mathrm{tr}_P\nabla^2_{\hat{\mathrm{g}}}K_\lambda(\hat r_a)\leq (-\lambda-C\hat r_a)|K'_{\lambda}(\hat r_a)|$. By smoothness of $h_t$ in $t$ we have $|h_t-h_0|=O(|t|)$, $|\mathrm{g}-\hat{\mathrm{g}}|_{\hat{\mathrm{g}}}\leq C|t|$, $|\nabla^\mathrm{g}-\nabla^{\hat{\mathrm{g}}}|_{\hat{\mathrm{g}}}\leq C$.
	Then,
	\[
